Gap crossing number
Definition: In a gap drawing each crossing is assigned to one of the two crossing edges.
    The gap crossing number of a gap drawing is the largest number of crossings assigned
    to any edge in the drawing. The gap crossing number of a graph is the smallest gap
    crossing number of any gap drawing of the graph.
Reference: Based on Bae et al. [94].
Comments: Bae et al. introduce the notion of k -gap planar graphs which are the graphs
    with gap-crossing number at most k ; this notion combines the local crossing number
    (or k -planar graphs) with the method of edge casing (see Remark 7), in which one of
    the edges in a crossing is erased close to the crossing, leading to a gap in the edge.
    Van Beusekom, Parada, and Speckmann [132] introduce crk-gap (G), the smallest
    number of crossings in any k -gap planar drawing of G and study the crossing ratio
    crk-gap (G)/ cr(G) for all k -gap planar graphs. One could also imagine a rectilinear
    gap crossing number.
Complexity: NP-complete [94], even for testing gap crossing number 1 and the rotation
    system is given. Fixed parameter tractable even on surfaces [245].
Relationships: The local crossing number is at least twice the gap crossing number,
    and also is a lower bound on the gap crossing number [94]. For every k ⩾ 1
    there is a graph G with gap crossing number 1 and lcr(G) ⩾ k [94]. If G has gap
    crossing number at most k , then m = O(k 1/2 n) [94, 742], for k = 1, the bound is
    m ⩽ 5n − 10 [94].
Values: The gap crossing number of Kn is at most 1 if and only if n ⩽ 8. Some values
    of complete bipartite graphs are determined in [91, 742].
Open Questions: Does K3,13 have gap crossing number at most 1 [91, 742]?
Also see: Local crossing number.


Genus crossing number
Definition: The genus crossing number of a drawing D of a graph G is the number of
    points  in which edges cross each other (that is, we count this point only once, not
     k
         times for k edges passing through it); we do not allow edges to touch in the
       
     2
    shared point, but we do allow self-crossings of an edge (so an edge can pass through
    the same crossing point multiple times at no additional cost). The genus crossing
    number of a graph G, gcr(G), is the smallest number of crossing points in a drawing
    of G.
Reference: Mohar [616].
Comments: Mohar proves that the genus crossing number equals the non-orientable
    genus of a graph. He conjectures that gcr(G) = dcr(G) [616].
Complexity: NP-complete [616] (since Carsten Thomassen showed that determining
    the non-orientable genus of a graph is NP-complete [618]).
Relationships: gcr(G) ⩽ mcr(G) since gcr is minor-monotone. There are graphs for
    which gcr(G) < mcr(G) [616]. Also, gcr(G) ⩽ dcr(G) by denition.


the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                   58
Values: Exact results for the non-orientable genus of Km and Km,n were given by Ringel,
    see [308] for a discussion.
Open Questions: Is Mohar's conjecture that gcr(G) = dcr(G) true? For partial positive
    results, see [739]; the stronger conjecture that dcr(G) = gcr(G) with the same
    embedding scheme fails, even for 1-vertex graphs [739] and for loopless 2-vertex
    graphs [362].
Also see: Degenerate crossing number.

Geodesic crossing number
Definition: The geodesic crossing number, crΣ (G), on a metric surface Σ, is the smallest
    number of crossings in a drawing of G on Σ where each edge is represented by a
    geodesic (with respect to the metric) in Σ. 100 Special cases include the rectilinear
    crossing number, where Σ is the plane with the Euclidean metric (in which case we
    write cr), the spherical (geodesic) crossing number [571, 622, 823], where Σ is the
    unit ball S 2 in three-dimensional Euclidean space, and the toroidal geodesic crossing
    number, where Σ is a (geometric) torus in three-dimensional Euclidean space.
Reference: Guy, Jenkyns, Schaer [418], also Harary, Hill [426].
Comments: The spherical geodesic crossing number of complete graphs is discussed by
    Harary and Hill [426]. Moon [621, 622], also see [40, 614, 615], studies the number of
    crossings in a random geodesic drawing of Kn on the sphere (vertices are picked at
    random, edges are shortest arcs). In [658] it was called . Both spherical and toroidal
    geodesic crossing numbers are introduced and studied explicitly in [418]. It is not
    clear from the paper whether the authors believe that the toroidal geodesic crossing
    number is independent of the actual geometric shape of the torus; they concentrate
    on a single model (the unit square with opposite sides identied). They explicitly
    equate the rectilinear crossing number with the geodesic crossing number, even
    though Harary and Hill [426] had earlier realized that K8 has a geodesic drawing
    on the sphere with at most 18 crossings, whereas cr(K8 ) = 19 was unproven, but
    expected to be true at the time. Guy [409, 410] later realized that the spherical
    crossing number of Kn is at most Z(n) = X(n)X(n−2)/4, where X(n) = ⌊n/2⌋⌊(n−
    1)/2⌋; this again shows that the spherical crossing number of K8 is at most 18.
    Since he could also show that cr(K8 ) = 19 (also Barton [110] and Singer [763]), this
    separates rectilinear and spherical crossing number. It is not clear whether all papers
    discussing geodesic crossing numbers distinguish between shortest arcs and geodesics
    (exceptions are [622, 823] which explicitly dene the geodesic crossing number in
    terms of shortest arcs rather than geodesics). This question is studied in [491], which
    uses the term shortest path crossing number. Another way to resolve the issue is to
    require the vertices to be in general position, which leads to the spherical arc crossing
    number. Elkies [307] extends Moon's work by studying random geodesic drawings
    on the projective plane and the torus. For a connection between the spherical
  100 Intuitively, geodesics are locally shortest arcs. A geodesic is not necessarily a shortest arc between
two points on a surface, and it need not be unique, as the example of antipodal points on the sphere
shows.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                    59
    geodesic crossing number and counting regular triangulations of higher-dimensional
    pointsets, see [344].
Complexity: Open, but likely to be ∃R-hard (and in ∃R assuming the metric is natu-
    ral), see [729] for ∃R.
Relationships: crS 2 (G) ⩽ cr(G) (a suciently small drawing of G will realize this).
    The spherical arc crossing number lies between crS 2 (G) and cr(G).
Values: crS 2 (Kn ) ⩽ Z(n), where Z(n) = X(n)X(n − 2)/4, with X(n) = ⌊n/2⌋⌊(n −
    1)/2⌋, is Zarankiewicz's function, the conjectured upper bound on cr(Kn ) [409, 615,
    706, 823]. 101 It is known that for the unit sphere, crS 2 (Kn ) > 0.996 Z(n) [104].
    Extending Moon's work    on randomized geodesic constructions,nElkies showed that
    crN1 (Kn ) ⩽ (3/π ) 4 for n ⩾ 15, and crS1 (Kn ) ⩽ (22/81) 4 [307] for natural
                        2 n

    geometric models of N1 and S1 . Let s(r, n) be the expected number of crossings
    in a random geodesic drawing of a complete, balanced r-partite graph Knr . Then
                                                               2 −r)
    limn→∞ s(r, n)/ max-cr(Knr ) = ζ(r), where ζ(r) := 8(r3(r2 +r−3) , see [385].
Open Questions: Does crS 2 (Kn ) = Z(n) hold?      For the closely related spherical arc
    crossing number, this was shown by Streltsova and Wagner [779, 780]. ▼ Is there a
    Fary theorem for metric surfaces? That is, is it true that crΣ (G) = 0 implies that
    crΣ (G) = 0 for a surface Σ equipped with a natural metric? 102 There are Fary-

    the geodesic crossing number is dened in terms of geodesics or shortest arcs? 103
    Shortest arcs can cross more than once (without overlapping) in some surfaces; are
    there examples of graphs for which every optimal geodesic (or shortest arc) drawing
    requires some edges to cross more than once?.104

Grid crossing number
Definition: A d-dimensional grid drawing of a graph G is a geometric (straight-line)
    embedding of G into Nd , that is, vertices are assigned to points in Nd , edges are
    straight-line segments between their endpoints, and we require that no vertex lies
    on an edge, unless it is an endpoint of that edge. The volume of a d-dimensional grid
    drawing of G is the volume of a smallest axis-parallel box containing all points of the
    grid drawing. The d-dimensional volume N grid crossing number of G, cr# (G, N, d)
    is the smallest number of crossings in a d-dimensional grid drawing of G of volume
    at most N .
Reference: Based on Dean [264], Dujmovi¢, Morin, Sheer [287], Swamy [787, Q5] for
    name.
Comments: Dean [264] asked whether it is hard to tell whether a given graph has a cross-
    ing minimal-drawing on an n×n-grid, where n is part of the input. Dujmovi¢, Morin,
    Sheer [287] introduce the crossing number of a grid graph (what we called a grid
    drawing), which they write cr(G), G being a grid graph/drawing, and then study the
    crossing number of that, in particular, the parameter crd (N, m) = min{cr(G) : G is
    a d-dimensional grid drawing of a graph with m edges and volume at most N }, which
    is quite natural, since their main goal is a crossing lemma result for grid graphs.
    They point to several previous papers that have studied grid embeddings, that is,
    grid drawings without crossings (also called non-crossing grid graphs in the litera-
    ture), but theirs seems to be the rst paper to study the crossing number notion.
    The 2-dimensional grid crossing number is a renement of the rectilinear crossing
    number. It is well-known that cr(G) can be realized on a grid of double exponential
    size and that grids of that size are necessary for some graphs (Bienstock [135]). It
    is in this context that Swamy [787] coined the term grid crossing number.
Complexity: NP-complete for d = 2. 105
Relationships: cr(G) ⩽ cr# (G, N, 2) (by denition), and cr(G) = cr# (G, N, 2) for N =
       cn
    22 for some c > 0 106 and there are graphs for which cr(G) < cr# (G, N, 2) if N =
       dn
    22 for some 0 < d [135]. cr# (G, N, 2) = Θ(m3 /N 2 ) for m ⩾ 4N (follows from [34]
    as observed in [287]), cr# (G, N, 3) = Ω(m2 /N log log(m/N )) for m ⩾ 2(2d − 1)N ,
    cr# (G, N, 3) = Ω(m2 /N log(m/N )), and cr# (G, N, d) = Ω(m2 /N ) [287].
Values: cr(G, (n − 2)2 , 2) = 0 for planar graphs G [743]. cr(G, O(n), 3) = 0 for pla-
    nar graphs [827]. For complete graphs, it is known that cr(Kn , 4n3 , 3) = 0, and
  105 Bienstock [135] showed that for every G there is a G′ with cr(G)        = cr(G′ ), where G′ is ob-

tained from G by subdividing each edge at most cn             times (for some xed c > 0).       We claim
                         ′    2
that cr(G) = cr# (G , cn , 2) which implies that computing cr# (G, N, 2) is NP-hard.           To see that
cr(G′ ) = cr# (G′ , cn2 , 2), take a cr-optimal drawing of G′ . Replace each crossing with a (very small)
C4 close to that crossing, so that the corners of C4 become the endpoints of the four half-edges meeting
at the crossing. Triangulate the drawing, keeping the C4 -faces empty; the resulting graph is 3-connected,
so by a result from [228], it has an embedding on the (n − 2) × (n − 2) grid in which all faces are convex.
In particular, we can replace each C4 by two diagonal edges, and remove all triangulation edges to obtain
                      ′
a grid drawing of G .

see [729], for example.

Independent algebraic crossing number
Definition: The independent algebraic crossing number of G, iacr(G), is dened like
    acr(G) except that we do not count acr(e, f ) for adjacent edges e and f .
Reference: Tutte [812].
Comments: Tutte's paper Toward a Theory of Crossing Numbers is often cited claim-
    ing it (implicitly) contains all kinds of crossing number denitions. A look at the
    text shows that Tutte denes two crossing numbers: the standard crossing number
    (which he calls c(G)) and what we now call the independent algebraic crossing num-
    ber; his crossing chains count crossings algebraically, that is, over Z, not modulo 2
    as the odd crossing numbers do; moreover, he sets the coecients of pairs of adja-
    cent edges to 0 so they don't count. The crossing number he denes based on that,
    s(G), is iacr(G). Tutte writes: It is clear that c(G) ⩾ s(G). Does equality always
    hold? This question was answered in the negative by Tóth [807] who constructed
    a graph G with iacr(G) = acr(G) < cr(G).
Complexity: In NP (similar to algebraic crossing number). It is possible that NP-
    hardness can be achieved along similar lines as in [676].
Relationships: iacr(G) ⩽ acr(G) and iocr(G) ⩽ iacr(G) (by denition). It follows from
    results in [677] that there are graphs G for which iocr(G) < iacr(G).
Open Questions: Fulek and Kyn£l [366] ask whether iacrΣ = 0 implies that crΣ = 0
    for all graphs G and surfaces Σ. Since iacrΣ (G) ⩽ iocrΣ (G), this Z-version of the
    Hanani-Tutte theorem is true if the traditional Hanani-Tutte theorem holds for Σ.
    The traditional Hanani-Tutte theorem fails for surfaces Σ of orientable genus 4 and
    higher [366].
Also see: Algebraic crossing number, independent odd crossing number.

Independent crossing number
Definition: The independent crossing number of G, cr− (G), is the smallest number of
    crossings between pairs of independent edges in any drawing of G.
Reference: Pach, Tóth [667].
Comments: The rst explicit denition of the independent crossing number seems to be
    in Pach, Tóth [667]. Not counting crossings between adjacent edges is implicit in
    many early papers, and, for straight-line or geodesic drawings, entirely justied [622].
Complexity: NP-complete.
Relationships: pcr− (G) ⩽ cr− (G) ⩽ cr(G) (from denition). The spectrum of cr− has
    been studied for K5 , K3,3 , and K6 in [183]. 107
  107 Here, the spectrum of a graph G is the set of all values cr
                                                                − (D) of drawings D of G in which pairs
of independent edges cross at most once.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                               62
Open Questions: It is not known whether cr− (G) < cr(G) is possible.       This would
       follow from a separation of the corresponding monotone crossing numbers [368].

Independent odd crossing number
Definition: The independent odd crossing number of G, iocr(G), is the smallest number
    of independent pairs of edges crossing an odd number of times in any drawing of G.
Reference: Székely [788].
Comments: This variant seems to have been introduced and named by Székely. He at-
    tributes it to Tutte [812], but Tutte really dened the independent algebraic crossing
    number. 108
Complexity: NP-complete [676] even if restricted to cubic graphs.
Relationships: iocr(G) ⩽ ocr(G) for all graphs G (by denition). iocr(G) = ocr(G) =
    cr(G) for iocr(G) ⩽ 2 [680], generalizing the Hanani-Tutte theorem (Footnote          7).
    There are graphs G for which iocr(G) < ocr(G) [368]. cr(G) ⩽       2 iocr(G)
                                                                                  [680]; this
                                                                                
                                                                           2
    implies that ocr, acr, pcr, cr and all their + and − variants are within a square
    of each other. There is a crossing lemma: iocr(G) ⩾ 1/64 m3 /n2 . 109 There are
    algebraic suciency criteria for iocr(G) = cr(G) [789]. iocr(G) ⩾ sk(G). 110 For
    surfaces other than the sphere, the only known result is that iocrN1 (G) = 0 implies
    crN1 (G) = 0 [673]. A smallest counterexample to iocrΣ (G) = 0 implying crΣ (G) = 0
    must be 2-connected [736]. There is a graph G with iocrΣ (G) = 0 and crΣ (G) > 0
    for any surface Σ of genus at least four [366]. See Remark 3 for a discussion of
    crossing lemmas for iocrΣ .
Values: iocr(GP(12, 4)) = 4, where GP(12, 4) is the generalized Petersen graph [353]. 111
Open Questions: Are there interesting graphs G for which cr(G) = sk(G)? For each
    such G we have iocr(G) = cr(G) (settling all intermediate crossing numbers, such as
    pcr and cr− as well). ▼ Using the notion of edge distance, we can express iocr(G) > 0
    as saying that drawings of G always contain two edges of distance at least one that
    cross oddly. In [498] it is shown that the Petersen graph always contains two edges at
    distance one that cross oddly, and the Heawood graph contains two edges at distance
    two that cross oddly. Are there general patterns on how crossings distribute between
    dierent edge distances?
Also see: Odd crossing number, independent algebraic crossing number (under alge-
    braic crossing number), monotone crossing number (for monotone version).
  108 Parity is only mentioned in one short passage in Tutte's paper [812], and that occurs when he
observes that for two edges e and f , acr(e, f ) ≡ cr(e, f ) mod 2.
  109 For a proof, see the section on crossing lemma variants in Section 1.
  110 Not claimed anywhere, but easy: If iocr(G) = k , we can remove at most k edges, so that iocr(G) = 0,
implying cr(G) = 0, and the graph is planar.
  111 As so far the only non-trivial result for iocr, this deserves some comment. The article [353] actually
shows (though it doesn't claim so) that sk(GP(12, 4)) ⩾ 4. Since iocr is lower-bounded by sk, and there is
a drawing of GP(12, 4) with four crossings, the result follows. The same paper determines cr(GP(3k, k))
for all k , but the inductive step seems to use cr in an essential way.

Independent pair crossing number. See pair crossing number.

Joint crossing numbers
Definition: Suppose G1 and G2 are graphs embedded in the same surface Σ; a joint
      embedding of G1 and G2 is a simultaneous embedding of homeomorphic copies of
    G1 and G2 in which the only shared points between G1 and G2 are (transversal)
    crossings of an edge of G1 with an edge of G2 ; if we restrict the homeomorphisms to
    be orientation-preserving, we speak of a joint orientation-preserving embedding. If
    we restrict the homeomorphisms so that all vertices of G1 lie in a face of G2 and vice
    versa, we call the joint embedding single-faced. The (joint) (homeomorphic) cross-
    ing number of G1 and G2 , cr(G1 , G2 ), is the smallest number of crossings in any joint
    embedding of G1 and G2 in Σ, the oriented crossing number, →        −
                                                                        cr(G1 , G2 ) of G1 and
    G2 , is the smallest number of crossings in any joint orientation-preserving embed-
    ding of G1 and G2 . The single-faced crossing number, crsf (G1 , G2 ), is the smallest
    number of crossings in any single-faced joint embedding of G1 and G2 . Similarly,
    →
    −
    cr sf (G1 , G2 ), is the single-faced oriented crossing number. We can relax the notion of
    joint embedding to a diagonal embedding by allowing vertices of G1 to coincide with
    vertices of G2 and edges of G1 to coincide with edges of G2 . The smallest number of
    crossings in a diagonal embedding is the diagonal crossing number, cr∆ (G1 , G2 ). If
    we want to emphasize the underlying surface, we write cr(G1 , G2 ; Σ), for example.
    If instead of embedded graphs G1 , G2 we have abstract topological graphs that are
    embeddable in Σ, we can still dene the (joint) crossing number and the diagonal
    crossing number of G1 and G2 by additionally minimizing over all embeddings of
    G1 and G2 . Richter and Salazar [702] suggest the notation cr(ϕ1 (G1 ), ϕ2 (G2 )) for
    the embedded graph variant (ϕi (Gi ) is a class of homeomorphic embeddings of Gi ),
    Hlin¥ný and Salazar [465] suggested the name joint homeomorphic crossing number
    for this case to distinguish it from the topological case; we will rely on context.
Reference: Negami [635, 636]. Also, Archdeacon, Bonnington [64], and Richter, Salazar
    [702].
Comments: Joint crossing numbers, that is crossings numbers of pairs of (embedded)
    graphs were rst introduced by Negami [635, 636]. Archdeacon and Bonnington [64]
    restrict joint embeddings to orientation-preserving homeomorphisms, so their joint
    crossing number is what Negami called the oriented crossing number. Negami sim-
    ply uses crossing number for the joint crossing number. Richter and Salazar [702]
    explicitly dene the single-faced crossing number which is implicit in Archdeacon,
    Bonnington [64]. As examples for values of joint crossing numbers, Negami gives
    cr(K5 , K3,3 ; S1 ) = 2 and cr∆ (K5 , K3,3 ; S1 ) = 0. Since G1 and G2 are both required
    to be embeddable on Σ, the crossing number of pairs is always 0 for the plane.
Complexity: Joint crossing number (both homeomorphic and topological version), and
    joint oriented crossing number are NP-complete, for any orientable surface of genus
    at least 3, even for simple, 3-connected graphs [457, 465]. As [465] point out, an

the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                      64
    earlier result by Archdeacon and Bonnington [64, Theorem 2.2] implies that the
    joint homeomorphic crossing number of two graphs on the projective plane can be
    solved in polynomial time. Both the homeomorphic and the topological version are
    xed-parameter tractable [245].
Relationships: cr∆ (G1 , G2 ) ⩽ cr(G1 , G2 ) (from denition). If γ(Σ) is the (orientable
    or non-orientable) genus of Σ, then →     −
                                              cr(G1 , G2 ; Σ) ⩽ 4γ(Σ)|E(G1 )| · |E(G2 )|, and
    →
    −
    cr(G1 , G2 ; S1 ) ⩽ 2/3|E(G1 )| · |E(G2 )| [64, 363, 635]. 112
Values: cr(G1 , G2 ; Sn ) = 2n if both G1 and G2 are 2-cell embedded on Sn so that each
    embedding has a single face [845].
Open Questions: Negami [635] conjectures that cr(G1 , G2 ) ⩽ c|E(G1 )| · |E(G2 )| for
    some constant c independent of Σ; Archdeacon and Bonnington [64] believe this
    conjecture to be false. They conjectured that →    −
                                                       cr(G1 , G2 ) ⩽ cΣ · →
                                                                           −
                                                                           cr sf (G1 , G2 ) for em-
    bedded graphs G1 and G2 which was shown to be false by Richter and Salazar [702]
    (who suggest a revised conjecture).
Also see: Simultaneous crossing number. Red/blue crossing number.

k-layer crossing number
Definition: A leveling of a graph G = (V, E) is a mapping from V to {1, . . . , k}, assign-
    ing each vertex a level. The leveling is proper if all edges of G are between vertices at
    adjacent levels. A layered drawing of a properly leveled (layered) graph is a drawing
    in which the vertices are placed on k parallel lines, with vertices in layer i assigned
    to the ith line, and edges are drawn as straight-line segments. The k -layer crossing
    number of a layered graph is the smallest number of crossings in a k -layer drawing
    of the graph.
Reference: Wareld [829], Sugiyama, Tagawa, Toda [784], Shahrokhi, Vr´o [758].
Comments: Shahrokhi and Vr´o [758] introduced (and named) the 3-layer crossing num-
    ber, but as a crossing minimization problem the k -layer crossing number is already
    present in papers by Wareld [829] and Sugiyama, Tagawa, and Toda [784]; these
    earlier papers write K(M ) for the layered crossing number of a leveled graph rep-
    resented by a matrix M . The 2-layer crossing number is just the bipartite crossing
    number. May and Szkatuªa [595] dened the p-partite crossing number, ν p for p-
    partite graphs: the vertices of each part are drawn on one of p parallel lines, and
    a subdivision vertex is added whenever an edge crosses a line, so this corresponds
    to the k -layer crossing number of a properly leveled graph. Di Bartolomeo, Lang,
    and Dunne [269] implement an algorithm for maximizing the number of crossings
    in a k -layer drawing.     Layered crossing numbers are similar to leveled crossing
                                113


    numbers, except that for the layered crossing numbers edges have to be realized as
    straight-line segments (rather than just being monotone); if the leveling is proper,
    the leveled and layered crossing numbers coincide. Leveling a graph imposes a lin-
    ear structure on the graph. One could also imagine allowing other structures, for
  112 The argument for Theorem 1 in [635] contains a gap which is xed in [363].
  113 They do so tongue-in-cheek, but one could study the maximum k -layer crossing number extending
the maximum bipartite crossing number.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                            65
    example trees [686], or cycles as in the cyclic level crossing number. Wotzlaw, Speck-
    enmeyer and Porschen [842] consider the case in which the ordering of the vertices
    in each layer is restricted by a tree (a generalization of the tanglegram problem, also
    see the comment in the entry on the bipartite crossing number).
Complexity: NP-complete [378], even for trees [449]. 114 Can be approximated to within
    a factor of O(log n) in polynomial time [758]. The embeddability problem can be
    decided in polynomial time and this remains true if the ordering of vertices in each
    layer is constrained by trees [842].
Relationships: The k -layer crossing number of G is at least cr(G) and it can be strictly
    greater than cr(G). The leveled crossing number is a lower bound on the k -layer
    crossing number.
Values: There is an upper bound on the k -layer crossing number of Lk       n , the complete
    balanced proper layered graph on k layers [324].
Open Questions: If a graph has leveled crossing number zero, that is, if it has a mono-
    tone leveled embedding, it has an embedding in which all edges are straight-line
    segments [300, 666], though the area of the graph may increase exponentially [579].
    Are there leveled graphs for which the k -layer crossing number is strictly larger than
    the leveled crossing number?
Also see: Bipartite crossing number, leveled crossing number (under monotone crossing
    number), cyclic level crossing number.

k-planar crossing number
Definition: The k -planar crossing number, crk (G), of G = (V, E) is the minimum of
    Pk                                                                          Sk
       i=1 cr(Gi ) , where the minimum  is taken  over all G i = (V, E i ) with  i=1 Ei = E .
    The special case cr2 is also known as the biplanar crossing number. If we restrict
    the drawings to be rectilinear, we get crk , the rectilinear k -planar crossing number.
    Given a rectilinear drawing
                           Pk     D of G, the geometric k -planar crossing number, crk (D),
    is the minimum of i=1 cr(Di ), where the minimum is taken over all Gi = (V, Ei )
    with ki=1 Ei = E , and Di is D restricted to Gi . The geometric k -planar crossing
           S
    number, crk (G), is the minimum of crk (D) over all rectilinear drawings D of G.                115


    The thickness, Θ(G), is the smallest k such that crk (G) = 0; similarly, the geometric
    thickness, Θ(G), is the smallest k such that crk (G) = 0. The local k -planar crossing
    number, lcrk (G), is the minimum of maxki=1 lcr(Gi ), where the minimum is taken
    over all Gi = (V, Ei ) with ki=1 Ei = E .

  114 The reduction by Garey and Johnson [378] is to bipartite multigraphs. The middle layer can be used
to replace multiple edges by parallel paths.

the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                66
Reference: Kodres [547], Owens [651], Shahrokhi, Sýkora, Székely, Vr´o [754], Pach,
    Székely, Tóth, Tóth [659]. The local, k -planar crossing number was introduced by
    Asplund, Do, Hamm, Jain [79].
Comments: Kodres [547] overlooked paper (see Remark 5) discusses (and implements)
    crossing minimization for biplanar and k -planar drawings of graphs (based on elec-
    tronic circuits); the paper shows that K7,7 is not biplanar and conjectures that
    cr2 (K7,7 ) = 4 (the correct value is 1 [252]). Owens [651] introduced the k -planar
    crossing number for arbitrary k , but focussed on the biplanar case, Shahrokhi,
    Sýkora, Székely, Vr´o introduced the rectilinear version. The k -planar crossing num-
    bers have also been called the multiplanar crossing numbers [538]. The k -planar
    crossing number should not be confused with the crossing number of a k -planar
    drawing which has only been studied for k = 1, where it is called the simple cross-
    ing number. The geometric variant was introduced by Pach, Székely, Tóth, Csaba,
    Tóth [659], rening Kainen's notion of real thickness [516]. The geometric variant
    has also been called the rectilinear k -colored crossing number in [30] and the geo-
    metric k -colored crossing number in [419]. If the geometric drawings were restricted
    to be convex, then one would get the k -page crossing number. The study of the
    k -planar crossing number is often motivated by linking it to questions of VLSI de-
    sign. Interestingly, there is a book from 1896 on electrical wiring which includes two
    diagrams illustrating how to draw connections without any crossings in two layers
    (die Verbindungen [können] in zwei übereinander liegenden Ebenen ohne Kreuzung
    gelegt werden) [70, Figures 51, 52]. In this context it may also be interesting to look
    at a grid (lattice)-version of these crossing numbers; the notion of lattice thickness
    was studied in [39].
Complexity: The k -planar crossing number is NP-complete, since the embedding prob-
    lem crk (G) = 0 is equivalent to the thickness of G being at most k and even for
    k = 2 this problem is NP-complete [587]. The rectilinear and geometric k -planar
    crossing numbers are ∃R-complete, since they coincide with cr for k = 1, but the
    case k ⩾ 2 is open, though likely to be ∃R-complete as well.

    (by denition). cr2 (G) ⩽ (3/8) cr(G) [253]. crk (G) ⩽ c cr(G) and crk (G) ⩽ c cr(G),
    where c = (2/k 2 − 1/k 3 ) (and c ⩾ 1/k 2 for some graphs G) [659]. It has been
    announced that the upper bound is c = 1/k 2 (1 + o(1)) [78]. crk (G) ⩽ c cr(G) for
    c = 1/k (and c ⩾ 1/k 2 for some graphs G) [659], and crk (G) ⩽ (1/k) cr(G) − c,
    k ⩾ 2, for suciently dense graphs [30, 323]. crk (G) ⩽ bkcr2k (G). 116 There is a

Local crossing number
Definition: The local crossing number of a drawing D of a graph G, lcr(D), is the largest
    number of crossings on any edge of G. The local crossing number of G, lcr(G), is the
    minimum of lcr(D) over all drawings of G. Dene the simple local crossing number
    lcr∗ (G) as the minimum of lcr(D) over all intersection-simple drawings D of G (every
    two edges intersect at most once). For the local crossing number on a surface Σ, we
    write lcrΣ . If we count multiple crossings only once, we get the (simple) degenerate
    local crossing number. If we maximize lcr(D) over all intersection-simple drawings
    D of G, we obtain max- lcr(G), the maximum local crossing number. If we restrict
    drawings to be straight-line, we get the rectilinear local crossing number, lcr(G).
Reference: Kainen [516]. Also, Ringel [712], Guy, Jenkyns, Schaer [418]. For the simple
    local crossing number, see Schumacher [746] and Pach, Tóth [664]. The simple
    degenerate local crossing number was introduced by Eggleton [305]. The maximum
    local crossing number is based on a paper by Harborth [432]. The rectilinear local
  117 The fact that K
                     9 is not biplanar was rst shown in [111, 811]. A much simper proof is now avail-
able [138].
  118 The authors ask a more general question, which is also of interest: nd bounds for a function k(c)
so that cr(G) ⩽ k(c) implies that cr2 (G) ⩽ c.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                68
   crossing number seems to have rst been mentioned in an earlier version of this
   survey.
Comments: The local crossing number was rst introduced by Gerhard Ringel in lec-
   tures and conversations in the 1960s [412, 506]. Guy, Jenkyns, and Schaer [418]
   dene the local toroidal crossing number, the local crossing number on a torus, lcrS1 .
   Kainen [516] introduces the local crossing number on arbitrary surfaces, and also
   credits Ringel [712]. Ringel's paper shows that a graph with at most one crossing
   per edge can be 7-colored,119 but Ringel doesn't name the local crossing number ex-
   plicitly in this paper. Graphs that can be drawn with at most one crossing per edge
   were later called 1-embeddable (Ringel [710]), 1-planar 120 (Schumacher [747]) and
   even simple, on occasion [168]; the drawn graph has been called 1-immersed [556].
   Kainen [514] considered the local crossing number on arbitrary surfaces, he shows
   that ΘΣ (G) ⩽ 1 + lcrΣ (G), with ΘΣ (G) being the thickness of G on surface Σ.
   Cimikowski [237] in his denition of local crossing number restricts drawings to be
   cr-minimal. It is easy to see that this leads to a dierent notion of local crossing
   number. Harary, Kainen, and Schwenk [428] gave as an example W5 □ K2 which has
   crossing number 2 and local crossing number 1, but any drawing of W5 □K2 realizing
   crossing number 2 has local crossing number at least 2. They conjecture that their
   example is the smallest possible. Eggleton [305] introduces a degenerate version of
   the local crossing number, that is, he counts multiple crossings as a single crossing
   (he also restricts drawings to be intersection-simple); he calls this variant the edge
   crossing number, not to be confused with the notion of edge crossing number we
   introduce. Eggleton shows that every outerplanar drawing in which each edge has at
   most one degenerate crossing is rectiable (realizable by straight-line segments and
   maintaining topological equivalence). Thomassen [802] calls lcr(D) the cross-index
   of D and studies conditions under which drawings D with lcr(D) ⩽ 1 are recti-
   able (realizable by straight-line segments, maintaining topological equivalence); this
   suggests the notion of geometric/straight-line 1-planarity [273, 482, 732], or, more
   generally, a rectilinear local crossing number, lcr, called crossing index in [570]. Schu-
   macher [746] uses the term n-embeddable for graphs G with lcr(G) ⩽ n, and claims
   that if we take a drawing D of G with lcr(D) ⩽ n and a minimal number of crossings,
   none of G's edges is crossing itself; two dierent edges with one vertex in common
   do not cross either, and two dierent edges without a vertex in common cross once
   at the most. The claim about self-crossings is obviously true, but the remaining
   two claims are false. See the graph in the margin for an example showing that ad-
   jacent edges can be forced to cross. 121 A slight modication of this example shows
  119 Borodin [156] shows that they can even be 6-colored, which is sharp, because K is 1-planar. Eppstein
                                                                                    6
and Huynh [316] point out that the chromatic number of graphs with local crossing number k is of order
  √
Θ( k).
  120 Not to be confused with the notion of k -planarity in the multi-planar crossing number.
  121 This was also observed, without detailed proof, in [654, Figure 1]. Some explanation of our example:
consider a drawing of the graph with lcr(D) ⩽ 4 in which the outer face is empty, in particular, the edges
of the outer cycle are free of crossings. Then it is easy to argue that the two adjacent left/right edges
have to cross in D . Here is how we enforce that the outer face is empty: add a new vertex and connect



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                  69
    that two edges can be forced to cross an arbitrary number of times in an lcr-optimal
    drawing. One could ask for an upper bound on the minimum number of crossings
    in a drawing D of a graph with lcr(D) ⩽ n. For n = 1 this yields the simple cross-
    ing number. Pach and Tóth [664] study the parameter we called the simple local
    crossing number without naming it. Bodlaender and Grigoriev [398] rediscovered
    the local crossing number, calling it crossing parameter. In a later paper, Grigoriev,
    Koutsonas, and Thilikios [399] use the term ξ -nearly planar for graphs with local
    crossing number at most ξ , and give an equivalent structural characterization of
    these graphs. For a convex (our outerplanar) version see the local convex crossing
    number (under convex crossing number). Feng, Ye, and Xu [343] suggest studying
    the minimal number of crossings along longest paths in a network (to model opti-
    cal router networks); this has a similar avor to the local crossing number, but is
    not strictly speaking a crossing number in our sense. With a similar motivation,
    Stallman and Gupta [772, 773] consider heuristics for the local crossing number of
    layered graphs, which they call the bottleneck crossing number 122 ; to be precise, they
    really dene what amounts to the local pair crossing number in which we minimize
    the largest number of edges crossing each edge (not the actual crossings), see the
    entry for pair crossing number. 123 Harborth [432] studies the largest number of
    crossings along an edge in (intersection)-simple drawings of complete (multipartite)
    graphs. Also see [483, Chapters 4-7]. In [139] a drawing is called min-k -planar if for
    every pair of crossing edges one of the edges is involved in at most k crossings; one
    could dene a crossing number based on this, maybe called the pairwise minimum
    local crossing number, but the authors do not do so; they do show a crossing lemma
    lower bound of the form Ω(m2 /n2 ) for this notion. Hlin¥ný and Ködmön [462, 464]
    point out that the denition of min-k -planarity is sensitive to whether drawings
    have to be simple or not. Combining the local crossing number with the edge casing
    drawing model of crossings yields the gap crossing number. Combining local and
    upward crossing number yields the upward local crossing number.
Complexity: Deciding whether lcr(G) ⩽ 1 is NP-complete, even if the graph is 3-
    connected, and a rotation system is known [84, 180, 398, 556], or a plane embedding
it to all vertices on the outer cycle. The vertices of this newly added star and the outer cycle form the
outer frame. For each edge uv in the outer frame, add 4|V (G)| + 1 = 89 parallel paths P3 between u and
v ; let the new graph be G′ and x a drawing D′ with lcr(D′ ) ⩽ 4 and minimizing cr(D′ ). We can assume
                                      ′
that no two adjacent edges cross in D (otherwise we're done). Let uv be an edge of the outer frame, and
xy be another edge. Then uv and xy cannot cross oddly: pick a cycle C containing xy , but not uv (if
xy also belongs to the outer frame, then the cycle can be completed with a P3 ). The cycle has length at
most |V (G)| = 22. Each of the 89 cycles of the form uv + P3 crosses C evenly, so if uv crosses xy oddly,
then each of the P3 must cross C oddly, so some edge in C has at least 89/22 > 4 crossings, contradicting
lcr(D′ ) ⩽ 4. So uv crosses every edge evenly, so it crosses either one, or two edges. One can reduce the
number of crossings in all cases, so uv and thus all edges of the outer frame are free of crossings.

ones presented above to separate local and simple local crossing numbers. The distinction was probably
not intended by the authors of [772, 773], since they also dene the crossing number as pcr. For layered
drawings there is no dierence between counting all local crossings or only counting local pair crossings.

Map crossing number
Definition: A map is a graph G = (V, E) and a surface Σ with boundary ∂Σ so that
    V ⊆ ∂Σ. In a drawing of G each edge is realized by a properly embedded arc (a
    connected curve that intersects ∂Σ in its endpoints only). The crossing number of
    the map is the smallest number of crossings in a drawing of the map. Similarly,
    one can dene odd, algebraic and pair crossing number for maps. We can introduce
    special names based on the number of boundary components of Σ: disk crossing
    number (one hole), annulus crossing number (two holes), pair of pants crossing
    number (three holes), and so on.
Reference: Pelsmajer, Schaefer, tefankovi£ [677].
Comments: The map crossing numbers were introduced in [677] to separate ocr from cr.
    One can turn every boundary component into a single vertex with rotation; as long
    as one is considering a crossing number variant in which adjacent crossings count
    the same as independent crossings, the crossing number notion does not change, so
    one can alternatively look at map crossing numbers as crossing numbers of graphs
    with rotation system; map crossing numbers can also be considered a special case
    of the constrained crossing number. If we allow vertices to arbitrarily move on
    their boundary component, the disk crossing number becomes the convex crossing
    number, and the annulus crossing number turns into the radial crossing number on
    two levels. (The general case does not seem to have been considered so far.)
Complexity: The disk crossing number can be computed in time Θ(m log m), where
    m = |E|; the annulus (algebraic) crossing number can be computed in polynomial
    time [676].    The complexity of computing the pair-of-paints crossing number is
                   131


    open. The general problem is NP-complete, since computation of the crossing
    number of a graph with a given rotation is NP-complete [676].
  131 Results in that paper are phrased for graphs with rotation systems.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                               73

    M for which 13 = ocr(M   ) < pcr(M ) = 15; if Σ has n boundary components, then
    cr(M ) ⩽ ocr(M ) 4 /5 [677].

Maximum crossing number
Definition: The maximum crossing number of a graph G, max-cr(G), is the largest
    number of crossings in any drawing of G in which every pair of edges has at most one
    point in common (including a shared endpoint; touching points are forbidden). 132
    The set of possible values {cr(D) : D is an intersection-simple drawing of G}, is the
    spectrum of G for cr.
Reference: Ringel [711], Grünbaum [404].
Comments: In a 1972 paper, Grünbaum [404] expresses surprise that max-cr(Kn ) and
    max-cr(Km,n ) have not been studied; he mentions max-cr(K4 ) = 1 and Saaty's claim
    that max-cr(Kn ) = n4 [722] which he calls probably true but unsubstantiated.
    Ringel had already settled this problem earlier [711]. This crossing number has
    also been called maximal crossing number [404]. One can try to relax the simplic-
    ity condition without allowing an innite number of crossings. One model allows
    independent edges to cross an arbitrary number of times, as long as they do not
    form empty lenses (bigons consisting of two subarcs of the edges that do not en-
    close a vertex). In this drawing model Kn has at most n! many crossings (and an
    exponential number of crossings is possible) [341]. 133
Complexity: NP-complete [214].
Relationships: max- cr(G) ⩽ max-cr(G) for all graphs G. max-cr(G) ⩽ θ(G), where
    θ(G) = m(m + 1) − v∈V deg2 (v) /2, with m = |E|, the thrackle bound [684], and
                          P              

    max-cr(G) ⩽ θ′ (G) := θ(G) − c4 + k4 , the sub-thrackle bound [708], where c4 is the
    number of C4 -subgraphs of G, and k4 the number of K4 -subgraphs of G.
                                                             Pk

    where n = ki=1 ni and k ⩾ 2 [438]. For trees T , max-cr(T ) = θ(T ), with θ(T ) as

    436, 841], see [176] for the spectrum of Cn . max-cr(Q3 ) = 34, where Q3 is the
    3-dimensional hypercube graph [437]. Asymptotically, max-cr(Wn ) is 5n2 /4 [448].

Maximum rectilinear crossing number
Definition: The maximum rectilinear crossing number of a graph G, max- cr(G), is the
    largest number of crossings in any simple straight-line drawing of G (by requiring
    the graph to be simple we avoid edge overlap). If we restrict drawings to be convex
    (all vertices on the boundary of a circle), we get the convex maximum rectilinear
    crossing number, here denoted by max- cr◦ (G). The set of possible values {cr(D) : D
    is a simple straight-line drawing of G}, is the spectrum of G for cr.
Reference: Grünbaum [404]. Also, Furry, Kleitman [369].
Comments: Originally dened by Grünbaum who mentions several results, including the
    calculation of max- cr(Cn ) due to Steinitz [778]. 134 Other names for this crossing
    number include maximal rectilinear crossing number [404] and obfuscation complex-
    ity [819]. Verbitsky writes obf(G) for max- cr and obf ◦ for max- cr◦ . Thürmann [803]
    considers a variant max- crh of max- cr parameterized by the number h of vertices
    that lie on the boundary of the convex hull of all vertices (but only for complete
    graphs).
Complexity: The maximum rectilinear crossing number is NP-hard [97], but not known
    to lie in NP. Can be approximated eciently to within a factor of 1/3 [97, 819]. For
    triangulations this bound can be improved to 56/39 [520]. The convex maximum
    rectilinear crossing number is NP-complete [97].
  134 Steinitz's result from 1923 was preceded by several incorrect or incomplete results, including a note
by Baltzer [105] who seems to have originated the problem in 1885; in turn, it was rediscovered multiple
times, e.g. in [369], for a (partial) survey see [406].

    denition).

Metro-line crossing number
Definition: Let G be a graph embedded in the plane, and L a set of paths (without
    repeated vertices) in G called lines. A routing of the lines orders all lines passing
    through an edge at each end of the edge. An edge crossing of two lines occurs if
    the ordering of the two lines at the two ends of some edge have switched. A vertex
    (station) is represented as a (convex) polygon with one side for each incident edge.
    The routing determines the order at each side of the station. If the entry and exit
    points of two lines alternate along the boundary of a station, a station crossing
    occurs; that is, the two lines have to cross within the station. The Metro-line
    crossing number of a particular routing of L in the embedding of G is the number
    of edge and station crossings of lines in edges. The Metro-line crossing number of L
    is the smallest Metro-line crossing number of any routing of L. 137
Reference: Based on Benkert, Nöllenburg, Uno, Wol [126], Argyriou, Bekos, Kauf-
    mann, Symvonis [68].
Comments: The concept of metro-line crossing minimization was introduced in Benkert,
    Nöllenburg, Uno, Wol [126], a more general model was suggested by Argyriou,
    Bekos, Kaufmann, Symvonis [68]. Both these papers consider the problem a crossing
    minimization problem and study it in various variants (e.g. stations have to be 2-
    sided or 4-sided or the end of lines may be forced to be in particular positions), so
    the metro-line crossing number dened above is just one possible variant.
Complexity: Optimizing the Metro-line crossing number of a single edge in G can be
    done in polynomial time [126] and there are NP-hard variants even if the underlying
    graph is a path [68] or a caterpillar [350]. There are polynomial-time and xed-
    parameter tractable cases for some variants [643].
Also see: Conuent crossing number, wire crossing number.


Minimum non-crossing edge number. See edge crossing number.
  137 One can distinguish between avoidable and unavoidable station crossings: two lines entering a station
through the same edge need not cross within the station, such a crossing can always be turned into an edge
crossing without increasing the Metro-line crossing number of the drawing. Since the unavoidable station
crossings can be computed in polynomial time, several papers restrict themselves to drawings without
avoidable station crossings, and then only count edge crossings. This also gives a more interesting variant
if one studies xed-parameter tractability.




the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                   77
Minor crossing number
Definition: The minor crossing number, mcr(G), of a graph G is the smallest crossing
    number of any graph having G as a minor. The major crossing number, Mcr(G), of
    a graph G is the largest crossing number of any minor of G. We write mcrΣ for the
    minor crossing number on surface Σ.
Reference: Bokal, Fijavº, Mohar [152].
Comments: The denition of the minor crossing number was motivated by an attempt
    to nd a crossing number that works well with minors, indeed it is minor-monotone
    by denition (the genus crossing number also addresses this issue), and is some-
    times called the minor monotone crossing number. Robertson and Seymour iden-
    tied the 41 forbidden minors of the set {G : mcr(G) ⩽ 1} [152]. Chimani and
    Gutwenger [215] introduce a variant mcrW (G), for W ⊆ V (G), in which only ver-
    tices in W are allowed to be expanded in the minor relationship; this allows them
    to draw connections to a hypergraph crossing number variant.
Complexity: NP-complete [461, 676]. 138 Testing mcr(G) ⩽ k is in polynomial time for
    any xed k , since the property is closed under minors. However, only for k = 1 is
    the set of forbidden minors known [152].
Relationships: mcrΣ (H) ⩽ mcrΣ (G) if H is a minor of G (from denition), mcrΣ (G) ⩽
    crΣ (G) ⩽ McrΣ (G) (from denition). crΣ (G) ⩽ ⌊∆/2⌋2 mcrΣ (G) [152], where ∆ is
    the maximum degree of G. mcrΣ (G) ⩾ (m − (3(n + eg(Σ)) + 6))/2, where eg(Σ) is
    the Euler genus of Σ and n = |V (G)|, m = |E(G)| [152]. There is a constant c(H)
    for every graph H so that mcr(G) ⩽ c(H)|V (G)| for every G that does not contain
    H as a minor [153].
Values: mcr(Kn ) is known for n ⩽ 8 [152]. There are asymptotic bounds for complete
    graphs, complete bipartite graphs and hypercubes [151, 152].
Also see: Genus crossing number.


Minor-monotone crossing number. Alternative name for minor crossing number.
Monotone crossing number. See monotone crossing numbers.
Monotone crossing numbers
Definition: A drawing is monotone if every vertical line in the plane intersects each
      edge at most once. The monotone crossing number of G, mon-cr(G), is the smallest
      number of crossings in a monotone drawing of G. If G is equipped with a preorder
      ⪯ (reexive and transitive) of its vertices we restrict the drawings of G to drawings
      which respect the preorder ⪯ in the sense that the total preorder created by the x-
      coordinates of the vertices extends ⪯. We write mon-cr⪯ (G) for the resulting (xed)
      monotone crossing number. If there is no danger of confusion, we will drop ⪯ in the
      notation. If ⪯ is the trivial preorder, then mon-cr⪯ is simply the monotone crossing
  138 Neither of those sources shows that the problem lies in NP. For that one needs to observe that for
every G there is a graph H so that mcr(G) = cr(H) and G can be obtained from H using a polynomial
(in size of G) number of contractions and deletions.




the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                78
      number mon-cr; if ⪯ is a total preorder we get the leveled crossing number 139 of
      which the bipartite crossing number and the k -layer crossing number are special
      cases. If ⪯ is a total order (at most one vertex per level, by anti-symmetry), we get
      the x-monotone crossing number. For a directed acyclic graph G with its induced
      preorder ⪯ we get the upward crossing number as mon-cr⪯ (G).
    For any crossing number notion ψ one can introduce the corresponding monotone
    version mon-ψ as above (with or without a given preorder), for example, one can talk
    about the monotone pair crossing number, mon-pcr or the monotone odd crossing
    number, mon-ocr.
Reference: Valtr [817], Fulek, Pelsmajer, Schaefer, tefankovi£ [368].
Comments: The monotone crossing number was introduced by Valtr [817] who also
    mentions monotone pair crossing number and monotone odd crossing number. The
    preorder versions are introduced in [368], but many of these problems are implicit in
    the crossing minimization problems studied in leveled (layered) graph drawing. The
    preorder version mon-cr⪯ suggested here is a general tool to unify many of these
    notions. One could imagine a bi-monotone crossing number in which orderings are
    prescribed both for the x and the y direction. Balko, Fulek, and Kyn£l [99] intro-
    duce the monotone odd + crossing number, mon-ocr+ (under the name monotone
    semisimple odd crossing number), and the monotone odd ± crossing number, ocr±
    (using the name monotone weakly semisimple odd crossing number).
Complexity: mon-cr(G) is NP-complete. 140 With two levels, crossing minimization is
    NP-complete (see bipartite crossing number for a discussion), even if the ordering
    of one level is given (one-sided crossing minimization) [298, 303]. Testing whether
    a directed graph has upward crossing number 0 is NP-complete [306].

    for all G [817]. mon-iocr(G) ⩽ mon-ocr(G)⩽ mon-ocr± (G) ⩽ mon-ocr+ (G) ⩽
    mon-cr(G) (denition). mon-cr(G) ⩽ 2 cr(G)   2

    mon-cr(G) ⩾ 7/6 cr(G) − 6 [665]. If there is a graph G with a linear order ⪯ of its
    vertices so that mon-ψ⪯ (G) < mon-ϕ⪯ (G) for ψ, ϕ ∈ {ocr, iocr, acr, iacr, pcr+ , pcr,
    pcr− , cr, cr− }, then there is a graph G′ for which ψ(G′ ) < ϕ(G′ ); there is a graph
    G with a linear order ⪯ of its vertices, so that mon-iocr⪯ (G) < mon-ocr⪯ (G) and
    consequently, there is a graph G′ so that iocr(G) < ocr(G) [368].

    of the monotone crossing number (each pair of edges may cross at most once). What

Multiplanar crossing number. See k-planar crossing number.
Nodal crossing number
Definition: Let crD (e) be the number of crossings involving e in a drawing D .         Let
    crD (v) be the sum of crD (e) over all e incident to v (in the literature, crD (v) is
    known as the responsibility of v in D). The nodal crossing number of a drawing D
    of a graph G, ncr(D), is the largest crD (v) over all vertices of G. The nodal crossing
    number of G, ncr(G), is the minimum of ncr(D) over all drawings of G. For the
    nodal crossing number on a surface Σ, we write ncrΣ .
Reference: Guy, Jenkyns, Schaer [418].
Comments: Graphs with nodal crossing number at most 1 are known as IC-planar
    (independent-crossing planar) graphs, which are well-studied [275]. In [832] the
    class of d-IC planar graphs is introduced which corresponds to graphs which have a
    drawing with local crossing number at most 1 and nodal crossing number at most
    d. The nodal toroidal crossing number, ncrS1 was introduced by Guy, Jenkyns,
    Schaer [418]; Guy [319, p.364] also referred to it as the vertical crossing number.
    In [213] graphs with nodal crossing number at most k are called k -vertex planar.
    Radermacher and Rutter consider a related parameter they call the co-crossing
    number of a vertex [696]. One can imagine a related notion of vertex-skewness in
    which we ask for the smallest number of vertices that can be removed to make a
    graph planar. This notion was rst mentioned by Harary [424], and called k -apex
    graphs in [132], also see Footnote 160. In [775] the non-increasing sequence of vertex
    responsibilities is called a crossing sequence.
Complexity: Deciding whether ncr(G) ⩽ 1 is NP-complete [160] (the result is stated
    for IC-planarity).
Relationships: lcr(G) ⩽ ncr(G) ⩽ cr(G) (by denition).
Values: ncrS1 (Kn ) is known for n ⩽ 9, and there are asymptotic results for ncrS1 (Kn )
    [418].
  141 The result remains true if the edges in the drawing are only x-bounded, that is, each edge lies
(horizontally) entirely between its endpoints.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                             80
Also see: Local crossing number, simple crossing number, vertex-skewness (under skew-
      ness).

Non-crossing edge number. See edge crossing number.
Odd crossing number
Definition: The odd crossing number of G, ocr(G), is the smallest number of pairs of
    edges crossing an odd number of times in any drawing of G. The Rule + variant of
    ocr is ocr+ (G), the smallest number of pairs of edges crossing an odd number of times
    in any drawing of G in which adjacent edges are forbidden to cross (called semisimple
    in [99], and star-simple in [341]). One can dene an intermediate variant in which
    adjacent edges have to cross evenly (such drawings are called weakly semisimple
    in [99]); denote this variant by ocr± . 142 Karl and Tóth [523] call a graph k -odd
    planar if it can be drawn so that every edge is crossed by at most k other edges an
    odd number of times (edges crossing an even number of times do not count). Based
    on that one can introduce the local odd crossing number of a graph G as the smallest
    k for which the graph is k -odd planar.
Reference: Pach, Tóth [668], also Levow [578].
Comments: First explicitly dened (and named) by Pach and Tóth [668], although
    Levow [578] deserves some credit; he realized that Tutte's algebraic theory of cross-
    ing number could be developed over binary elds (Wu developed a theory parallel
    to Tutte's over binary elds, but he didn't touch on the subject of crossing num-
    bers); Levow denes a parameter that could be algebraic or odd crossing number
    (or, indeed, an independent version). His denition is not precise enough to decide.
Complexity: NP-complete [668] and remains NP-complete if the graph is cubic or
    rotation system is given [676]. The problem is xed-parameter tractable [674].
Relationships: There is a crossing lemma, ocr(G) ⩾ 1/64 m3 /n2 for m > 4n [668].143
    For ocr± , a stronger bound is known: ocr± (G) ⩾ 1/54 m3 /n2 [523]. iocr(G) ⩽
    ocr(G) ⩽ ocr± ⩽ ocr+ (G) for all graphs G (by denition). ocr(G) ⩽ acr(G) ⩽ cr(G)
    (by denition).
                 √ ocr(G) = cr(G) if ocr(G) ⩽ 3 [678]. There are graphs for2 crwhich
    ocr(G) < ( 3/2 + o(1)) acr(G) = pcr(G) = cr(G)) [677]. ocrΣ (G) ⩽                 Σ (G)
                                                                                            

    for all surfaces Σ, and ocrΣ (G) = crΣ (G) if ocrΣ (G) ⩽ 2 for all surfaces Σ [679].

    graphs separating lpcr and lcr). If locr(G) ⩽ k , then m = |E(G)| ⩽ 32kn [523].
Also see: Independent odd crossing number, algebraic crossing number, monotone cross-
    ing number (for monotone version).
  142 The + rule for crossing numbers looks rather straightforward: we prohibit drawings in which adjacent
edges cross. One may ask, however, in what sense of the word cross? The standard interpretation is that
cr(e, f ) = 0 for all pairs of adjacent edges e and f . But why not require that ψ(e, f ) = 0 if we are
considering the crossing number ψ ? For cr and pcr (and cr, of course), this makes no dierence, but for
ocr and acr we get a new variant which we denote by ψ± [368]. By denition, ψ ⩽ ψ± ⩽ ψ+ .

Orchard crossing number
Definition: An orchard drawing of G is a straight-line drawing of G with vertices in
    general position to which are added straight (innite) lines through every pair of
    vertices. The orchard crossing number, orchard-cr(D), of an orchard drawing D of
    G is the total number of crossings between edges and lines (not counting the line
    an edge lies on). The orchard crossing number of G, orchard-cr(G), is the smallest
    orchard crossing number of any orchard drawing of G. The maximum orchard
    crossing number of G is the largest orchard crossing number of any orchard drawing
    of G.
Reference: Feder, Garber [335].
Comments: One can also imagine a pseudoline version of the orchard crossing number.
    Replacing lines with line segments in the denition of the orchard crossing number
    leads to the airport crossing number [320]. For the airport crossing number, a non-
    rectilinear version may be of interest as well.
Complexity: Open.
Relationships: cr(G) ⩽ orchard-cr(G)/2 [335] (since every edge crossing counts twice).
    The drawing maximizing the orchard crossing number of Kn realizes cr(Kn ) [335].
Values: orchard-cr(Kn ) = 2 4 , and the values of orchard-cr(K1,n ) and orchard-cr(Wn )
                              n
                                

    are known [335]; orchard-cr(Kn,n ) = 4n n3 [334]. Further results are in [333]. The
                                               

    maximum orchard crossing number of Km,n is known [335].
Also see: Rectilinear crossing number


Oriented crossing number. See joint crossing numbers.
Outerplanar crossing number. See convex crossing number.
Pair crossing number
Definition: The pair crossing number of G, pcr(G), is the smallest number of pairs
    of edges crossing in any drawing of G. The independent pair crossing number of
    G, pcr− (G), is the smallest number of pairs of independent edges crossing in any
    drawing of G. The Rule + variant of pcr is pcr+ (G), the smallest number of pairs
    of edges crossing in any drawing of G in which adjacent edges are forbidden to
    cross. The local pair crossing number of G, lpcr(G), is the smallest k so that G
    has a drawing in which every edge crosses at most k other edges (possibly multiple
    times).
Reference: Mohar (attributed in [548]), Pach, Tóth [667, 668], Ackerman, Schaefer [21]
    for lpcr.
Comments: According to Kolman and Matou²ek [548], the pair crossing number was rst
    explicitly introduced by Mohar who asked whether pcr = cr at an AMS Conference
    on topological graph theory in 1995. The rst mention in print seems to be by Pach
    and Tóth [668] (as the pairwise crossing number), who pointed out that crossing
    number is often dened as pair crossing number (whether intentionally or not),
    see Section 1 for a discussion. The independent pair crossing number was also


the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                82
    dened by Pach and Tóth [667]; Alon [44] and Tao and Vu [799] discuss the crossing
    lemma for the independent pair crossing number. The local pair crossing number
    was explicitly introduced by Ackerman, Schaefer [21], though there had been earlier
    implicit denitions of this notion [772, 773].
Complexity: The pair crossing number is NP-complete [668, 735] and remains NP-
    complete if the graph is cubic or rotation system is given [676]. The independent pair
    crossing number is also NP-complete. The pair crossing number is xed-parameter
    tractable [674].
Relationships: There is a crossing lemma for the independent pair crossing number,
    pcr− (G) ⩾ 1/64 m3 /n2 for m > 4n [44].144 For pcr+ a stronger lower bound
    is known, pcr+ (G) ⩾ 1/32.4m3 /n2 for m ⩾ 6.75n. ocr(G) ⩽ pcr(G) ⩽ cr(G),
    pcr− (G) ⩽ pcr(G) ⩽ pcr+ (G) for all G. If pcr− (G) = pcr(G), then pcr(G) =
    pcr+ (G). 145 There are graphs G for which ocr(G) < pcr(G) [677], indeed ocr(G) =
    acr(G) ⩽ 0.855 pcr(G) is possible [807]. cr(G) = O(pcr(G)3/2 ) [766]. The best
    previous bound was cr(G) = O(pcr(G)3/2 log pcr(G)) which follows from combin-
    ing a result by Matou²ek [592] (based on a proof by Tóth [804]), who showed
    that cr(G) = O(pcr(G)3/2 log2 pcr(G)) with a stronger string graph separator [574],
    see [730, Corollary 9.23] and, independently, [522]. Earlier bounds on cr in terms
    of pcr (using dierent techniques) are due to Valtr and Tóth [807, 817]. lpcr(G) ⩽
    lcr(G) (by denition), and there are graphs G for which lpcr(G) < lcr(G) (see
    Footnote 123), however, lpcr(G) = lcr(G) as long as lcr(G) ⩽ 2 [21],146 or G is
    suciently connected [525]. On the other hand Fox, Pach and Suk showed that
    lcr(G) ⩽ lpcr(G)(lpcr(G) + 1)(lpcr(G) + 2) [358] improving the earlier exponential
    bound from [737]. For a discussion of crossing lemmas for pcr and pcr− on surfaces,
    see Remark 3.
Open Questions: Mohar asked whether pcr = cr; Kolman and Matou²ek [548] called
    this question quite challenging [548].

Quasi crossing number
Definition: A drawing of a graph is quasi-plane if it does not contain three pairwise-
      crossing edges. The quasi(-plane) crossing number, quasi-cr(G), of G is the smallest
      number of triples of edges crossing pairwise in any drawing of the graph. If we re-
      strict the drawings to be intersection-simple, we get the simple quasi(-plane) crossing
      number, quasi-cr∗ (G).


the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                     84
Reference: Pitchanathan, Shannigrahi [690].
Comments: Pitchanathan and Shannigrahi [690] introduce the simple quasi crossing
    number, and use the notation cr3 (which we use for the 3-planar crossing number);
    we also distinguish the variant without the simplicity requirement. It's natural
    to extend both notions to the k -quasi(-plane) setting, yielding the k -quasi(-plane)
    crossing number and its simple variant.
Complexity: Open.
Relationships: There is a crossing lemma, quasi-cr (G) ⩾ c m5 /n4 . If lcr (G) ⩽ k ,
                                                       ∗                          ∗

    then quasi-cr∗k+1 (G) = 0 [52].
Values: quasi-cr (K10 ) = 0 [161]. quasi-cr (K11 ) = 4 [690].
                 ∗                          ∗

Open Questions: Is there a non-trivial upper bound on quasi-cr (Kn ) [690]. ▼ Is
                                                                        ∗

    quasi-cr(G) = quasi-cr∗ (G), or can quasi-cr∗ (G) be bounded in quasi-cr(G)? ▼ Is
    there a crossing lemma for quasi-cr? ▼ One could consider quasi-cr, the quasi recti-
    linear crossing number. Brandenburg [161] mentions in passing that quasi-cr(K10 ) >
    0, attributing it to [32]. What is the exact value, and how does quasi-cr relate to
    quasi-cr∗ ? ▼ It is a long-standing open question (e.g. [163, Problem 1, Section
    9.6]), whether quasi-cr∗k (G) = 0 implies that |E(G)| is linear, this is only known for
    k ⩽ 4 [360].
Also see: Crossing number of abstract topological graph.


Radial crossing number
Definition: A leveling of a graph G = (V, E) is a mapping from V         to {1, . . . , k}, as-
    signing each vertex a level. A radial drawing of G is a drawing in which vertices of
    level i are placed on the ith circle of k concentric circles; edges are required to be
    monotone in the sense that they cross every circle that is concentric with the level
    circles at most once. The radial crossing number of G is the smallest number of
    crossings in a radial drawing of G.
Reference: Bachmaier [87]. Richter, Thomassen [706]. Also, Northway [641].
Comments: Bachmaier [87] introduced the general concept of radial crossing number. If
    G is bipartite one can assign the vertices of each group to one of two circles, resulting
    in the bipartite cylindrical drawings introduced by Richter and Thomassen [706] to
    study the crossing number of Kn via bipartite cylindrical drawings of Kn,n ; there
    also is a concept of cylindrical crossing number for non-bipartite graphs. In a paper
    from 1940, Northway [641], suggested radial layouts and used the number of crossing
    lines as an aesthetic criterion.
Complexity: Radial level planarity can be tested in linear time [90]. For two levels,
    the radial crossing number is NP-complete (this easily follows from NP-hardness
    of the bipartite crossing number), as is the one-sided version (in which the ordering
    of the vertices on one level is xed) [87, 298, 303]. If orderings of vertices on both
    sides are xed, the problem is in polynomial time [676].147
  147 In this case, the radial crossing number turns into the annulus crossing number.




the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                       85
Relationships: The leveled crossing number of G is an upper bound on its radial cross-
    ing number. In particular, the bipartite crossing number, bcr, is an upper bound
    on radial crossing number with two levels (the upper bound may be strict, e.g. for
    K2,2 ).
Values: The radial crossing number of Kn,n on two levels is n 3 [706] (with each group
                                                              n
                                                               

    on a separate level). More recently, Sparks [768] showed that under the same re-
    strictions the radial crossing number of Km,n can be calculated.
Also see: Bipartite crossing number, leveled crossing number (under monotone cross-
    ing numbers), annulus crossing number (under map crossing number), cylindrical
    crossing number.

Rectilinear crossing number
Definition: The rectilinear crossing number of G,                 cr(G), is the smallest number of
    crossings in a straight-line drawing of G.
Reference: Harary, Hill [426].
Comments: The rectilinear crossing number for arbitrary graphs was introduced by
    Harary and Hill [426]. It is sometimes claimed that the rectilinear crossing number
    is also known as the linear or geometric(al) crossing number, but published evidence
    for that is slim. 148 De Avila-Martínez, Leaños, Medina [86], based on [4, 5, 191],
    introduce the m-symmetric rectilinear crossing number, which restricts the drawings
    to pointsets closed with respect to rotations by 2π/m. This crossing number is only
    dened for complete graphs.
Complexity: ∃R-complete [135, 263], see [729] for ∃R. Can be approximated to within
    an additive error of o(|G|4 ) in polynomial time [357]. The crossing number of a
    straight-line drawing of a graph can be computed in time O(n2 log n) [294].
Relationships: cr(G) ⩽ cr(G) for all graphs G, and inequality can be strict, e.g. 18 =
    cr(K8 ) < cr(K8 ) = 19 [110, 763]. 149 cr(G) = cr(G) if cr(G) ⩽ 3, but for every
    k there is a G such that cr(G) = 4 and cr(G) ⩾ k [136].150 cr(G) = cr(G) for
    maximal graphs of pathwidth 3 [134]. Also, cr(G) = O(∆ cr2 (G)), where ∆ is the
    maximum degree of G [137]; this was improved to cr(G) = O(∆ cr(G) log cr(G)) if
    |E| ⩾ 4|V | [753]. Wilf [837] points out that cr(G) ⩽ ρM/3, where M is the number
    of times 2K2 occurs as a subgraph in G, and ρ ≈ 0.38 is the rectilinear crossing
    constant (denition under values). 151 The rectilinear midrange crossing constant is
