Red/blue crossing number
Definition: Given graphs Gi = (Vi , Ei ), and point-sets Pi in the Euclidean plane with
    |Pi | = |Vi |, i ∈ {1, 2}, a red/blue drawing consists of straight-line embeddings of
    Gi on vertex set Pi , i ∈ {1, 2} (each graph by itself is free of crossings). The
    red/blue crossing number is the smallest number of crossings in a red/blue drawing
    (necessarily between edges of G1 , the red edges, and G2 , the blue edges; in other
    words, we count red/blue crossings). It is possible that the Gi have no red/blue
    drawing on the Pi , in which case we say that the red/blue crossing number is innite.
Reference: Based on Bereg, Jiang, Yang, Zhu [128].
Comments: Bereg, Jiang, Yang, Zhu [128] are interested in the smallest number of
    crossings between any two crossing-free, geometric spanning trees on P1 and P2 .
    However, they do go on to study the special case where the Gi are paths. A related
    notion is studied under the name chromatic crossing number in [49], also see [35].
Complexity: Testing whether the red/blue crossing number of two paths is 0 is NP-
    complete [128]. (Finding red/blue spanning trees with the minimum number of
    crossings can be solved in time O(n log n).)

the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                   88
Also see: Simultaneous crossing number, joint crossing numbers, geometric k -planar
       crossing number.

Right-angle crossing number
Definition: The right-angle crossing number of G is the smallest number of crossings in
    a straight-line drawing of G in which all pairs of crossing edges have to be orthogonal.
    If no such drawing exists, the right-angle crossing number is innite.
Reference: Based on Didimo, Eades, and Liotta [274].
Comments: Didimo, Eades, and Liotta [274] introduced the notion of RAC (Right Angle
    Crossing) drawing based on the aesthetic heuristic that drawings are easier to read
    if angles at crossings are large [490]. One can imagine a t-polygonal right-angle
    crossing number, in which each edge is allowed to consist of t line segments. Didimo,
    Eades, and Liotta [274] showed that every graph has nite 4-polygonal right-angle
    crossing number. A more relaxed version may only require angles to be at least
    some large α ⩽ 90, see [270, 289], or edges to be drawn as circular arcs [197].
Complexity: It is ∃R-complete [733] to decide whether the right-angle crossing number
    is nite (see [67] for earlier NP-hardness result). The problem remains hard even
    if there are at most eleven crossings per edge and in the xed embedding setting.
Relationships: The right-angle crossing number of G is at least cr(G). If G has nite
    right-angle crossing number, then m ⩽ 4n − 10 assuming that n ⩾ 4 [274]. 154
Open Questions: What is the complexity of deciding whether the right-angle crossing
    number is at most k for small values of k such as 1, 2, or 3 [733]? ▼ Does the
    right-angle crossing number remain ∃R-hard for graphs of bounded degree [733]?
    ▼ What is the complexity of the right-angle crossing number if one (or two) bends
    per edge are allowed, so called RAC1 (RAC2 ) drawings [733]? Does the right-angle
    crossing number problem remain ∃R-hard if we restrict it to drawings with local
    crossing number 1, that is, at most one crossing per edge?

Rotational crossing number. Crossing number of graph with rotation (or embedding)
system. See entry for crossing number.

Sequential crossing number
Definition: A sequential drawing of a family of graphs G = (Gi )k
                                                                i=1 , with Gi = (V, Ei ),
    is a family of drawings D = (Di )ki=1 , so that Di is a drawing of Gi , for all 1 ⩽ i ⩽ k
    and the drawing of Gi ∩ Gi+1 is thePsame in Di and Di+1 , for all 1 ⩽ i < k . The
    sequential crossing number of D is       1⩽i⩽k cr(Di ). The sequential crossing number
    of G is the smallest sequential crossing number of a sequential drawing of G .
Reference: Hamm [373, Sections 4.5, 5.4].
Complexity: NP-complete, since crossing number is the special case k = 1, remains
    NP-complete even if |Ei | ⩽ 5 for all 1 ⩽ i ⩽ k [373, Section 5.4], using [258].
Also see: Simultaneous crossing number.
  154 Linear edge bounds are also known for graphs with nite t-polygonal right-angle crossing number,
where t ∈ {2, 3} [69, 527].



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                              89
Simple crossing number
Definition: The simple crossing number of G, cr× (G), is the smallest number of cross-
    ings in any drawing of G in which every edge has at most one crossing. 155 If there is
    no such drawing, we let cr× (G) = ∞; the name simple crossing number conicts
    with the usual notion of a simple drawing (which only requires that every two edges
    cross at most once). 156 Kainen [514] called a drawing in which every edge has at
    most one crossing nearly planar, Ringel [710] called it a 1-embedding; the graphs
    with cr× (G) ⩽ 1 are called 1-planar [747].
Reference: Buchheim, Ebner, Jünger, Klau, Mutzel, Weiskircher [168].
Comments: Buchheim, Ebner, Jünger, Klau, Mutzel, Weiskircher [168] introduce this
    variant to simplify their integer linear program for crossing minimization; the use-
    fulness of the simple crossing number lies in the fact that every graph G has a
    subdivision G′ for which cr(G) = cr× (G′ ). 1-planar graphs can be 6-colored [156,
    712] (in principle) and 7-colored in linear time [203].
Complexity: Deciding whether cr× (G) < ∞ is NP-complete [398]. Deciding cr× (G) ⩽
    k for 1-planar graphs is also NP-complete, even if the graph is 3-connected, and a
    rotation system is given [84].
Relationships: cr× (G) < ∞ is equivalent to lcr(G) ⩽ 1. If cr× (G) < ∞, then m ⩽
    4n − 8 and cr× (G) ⩽ n − 2 [149]. 157 All suciently large 3-connected, 2-crossing
    critical graphs have simple crossing number 2 [150].
Also see: Local crossing number, simple crossing number of abstract topological graphs
    (under crossing number of abstract topological graphs).

Simple degenerate crossing number. See degenerate crossing number. Simple de-
generate local crossing number. See local crossing number.
Simple local crossing number. See local crossing number.
Simple quasi crossing number. See quasi crossing number.
Simultaneous crossing number
Definition: A simultaneous drawing of a family of graphs G
                                                                   Sk       Sk, with Gi =
                                                                    = (Gi )ki=1
       (Vi , Ei ), is a drawing of G = (V, E) with V = i=1 Vi and E = i=1 . In other
       words, vertices or edges that belong to more than one graph are drawn only once.
       There are two dierent types of crossings in the drawing of G: a proper crossing is
       a crossing between two edges e and f that belong to the same graph Gi for some
       i, otherwise the crossing is a phantom crossing. The simultaneous crossing number
       of G , scr(G), of a family of graphs G = (Gi )ki=1 is the smallest number of proper
  155 Ringel [712] already observed that crossings between two adjacent edges can always be removed in
such a drawing.
  156 Another possible name, the 1-planar crossing number, clashes with the k -planar crossing number
introduced by Owens. This is doubly unfortunate, since that name suggests a nice generalization beyond
1-planarity.
  157 The result that any 1-planar drawing of a graph G on n vertices has at most n − 2 crossings is
implicit in several papers, e.g. [149, 325], an explicit statement can be found in [256].




the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                              90
    crossings in any simultaneous drawing of G as dened above. A proper crossing
    of two edges e and f counts once for each graph Gi in which it occurs. A family
    of graphs is simultaneous planar if scr(G) = 0. If we restrict the drawings to be
    straight-line drawings, we get the simultaneous geometric crossing number of G, scr.
    If we restrict the drawings to be convex (all vertices on the boundary of a disk, all
    edges inside the disk), we get the convex simultaneous crossing number.
Reference: Chimani, Jünger, Schulz [219], He, S l gean,and Mäkinen [451].
Comments: The crossing number scr(G) was introduced in Chimani, Jünger, Schulz
    along with several minimization problems, including the minimization of phantom
    crossings in an scr-minimal drawing. Geiÿer [384] studies the number of phantom
    crossings in a simultaneous embedding of G (so no proper crossings are allowed).
    This could be called the simultaneously planar crossing number. Chimani, Jünger,
    Schulz also consider a weighted variant of scr(G) which is still restricted to counting
    only proper crossings. One could consider a more general variant in which phantom
    crossings are assigned weights. The restriction to drawings in which edges belonging
    to more than one graph are drawn only once is typically known as the simultaneous
    embedding with xed edges (SEFE) style (an unfortunate name). When dening the
    crossing number version, the xed edges epithet was dropped. One could consider
    dening a free version in which edges belonging to multiple graphs may be drawn
    dierently for each graph. Families of graphs on the same vertex set are known
    as multiplex networks in information visualization, and there is research on layout
    algorithms in that area [331]. The convex simultaneous crossing number is based on
    an observation by He, S l gean,and Mäkinen [451] which implies that it corresponds
    to a book drawing in which edges belonging to the same Gi are assigned to the same
    page. It extends the partitioned book crossing number; it is more powerful, since
    in an edge in a simultaneous drawing can belong to multiple graphs. In the SEFE
    model, Grilli [400] introduced what amounts to a local and local pair version of the
    simultaneous planar crossing number.
Complexity: NP-complete [219]. 158 Testing simultaneous planarity is NP-complete
    for three graphs (the complexity of testing simultaneous planarity of two graphs
    is open) [381]. The simultaneously planar crossing number is NP-complete [181,
    195, 384], as is the local simultaneous planar crossing number [400]. The convex
    simultaneous crossing number generalizes the convex crossing number and there-
    fore is NP-complete. Testing convex simultaneous planarity is NP-complete if the
    number of graphs k is not bounded [484]; it is open whether the problem remains
    NP-complete for xed k .
                                                                        Sk

    Sk
       i=1 Ei [219]. The number of phantom crossings in an scr-minimal drawing can
    be forced to be exponential [219], though it is not clear whether this is true for xed
    k ; the case k = 2 would be particularly interesting. The top picture in the margin

k > 1 [734].

Skewness
Definition: The skewness sk(G) of a graph G is the smallest number of edges whose
    removal from G leaves a planar graph. We write skΣ (G) for the smallest number of
    edges whose removal leaves a graph embeddable in the surface Σ.
Reference: Guy [413], also Harary [424].
Comments: As discussed in Example 1, skewness does not t our denition of crossing
    number, but it is included because of its close relationship with both the crossing
    and the edge crossing number. Harary [424], in a 1965 paper, asks how can one
    nd a set of c(G) edges whose removal results in a planar graph, where c(G) is
    the crossing number of G. In 1972, Guy [413] introduces skewness under a dierent
    name, writing J. Ch. Boland suggested, and Mrs. Sheehan named the idea of
    the slimming number, citing a 1967 Oberwolfach meeting for the planar version,
    and a 1969 Oberwolfach meeting for the surface version, which he calls the generic
    slimming number in the orientable case, and the characteristic slimming number in
    the non-orientable case. Kainen [513] writes µt for the skewness on an orientable
    surface Σ of genus t and calls the concept t-skewness; we will use skΣ . The use
    of skew for non-planar graphs probably traces back to Kuratowski's paper [118].
    One also occasionally nds the name removal number for skewness [540, 601]. The
    skewness of a graphs is also sometimes referred to as its ((non-) planar) edge deletion
    number [23, 329, 691]. Guy [413] used a bound that was rst explicitly stated and
    proved by Kainen [507], namely that skΣ (G) ⩾ m − g/(g − 2) (n − 2 + 2γ), where g
    is the girth of G and γ = γ(Σ) the genus of the surface Σ. This lower bound, and its
    special form for the plane, have been rediscovered several times [206, 687]. A graph
    with skewness at most k is sometimes called k -skew, though often the term is applied
    to a specic drawing of the graph. 1-skew graphs are also often called almost planar.
    Chia and Sim [210] call a graph π -skew if sk(G) = π(G) := ⌊m − g/(g − 2) (n − 2)⌋,
    where g is the girth of G.
       Skewness is often discussed in its equivalent form of nding a maximum planar
       subgraph of a graph [184, 835]. Finding a maximum induced planar subgraph
  159 In both examples, there are two graphs: green and red, and the black edges belong to both the green
and the red graph; the outer face is forced to be empty.   These examples also show that not allowing
adjacent or multiple phantom crossings can increase the simultaneous crossing number.       The obvious
generalizations of these examples, e.g. showing that two edges may be made to cross an arbitrary number
of times, are incorrect.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                 92
       corresponds to removing the smallest number of vertices from a graph making it
       planar. 160
       One could introduce a version of skewness which asks for the fewest number of edges
       that need to be removed to reduce the crossing number below a certain bound. The
       corresponding maximum-bounded-crossing subgraph problem was introduced and
       studied in [232].
    Skewness can be based on crossing numbers other than the standard crossing number
    (planarity) and the surface crossing number (surface embeddability). We are aware
    of three such variants, based on the convex crossing number, the bipartite crossing
    numbers, and a local version of the xed linear crossing number: Kainen [509]
    introduces the outerplanar skewness of a graph, which he writes as µ1 (G), to give
    a lower bound on bkcr1 (Cm □ Cn ); the same concept was called convex skewness
    in [42]. Starting with the bipartite crossing number, one obtains the notion of 2-
    level skewness (discussed under bipartite crossing number). The authors of [373,
    Section 5.1] study how many vertices in a xed 1-page drawing need to be removed
    to ensure that all edges have at most a given number of crossings; the version for
    k -page drawings is studied in [25].
Complexity: NP-complete [580],161 and remains NP-hard to approximate within some
    constant factor, even for cubic graphs [330]. 162 Testing sk(G) ⩽ k can be done
    in linear time for xed k [532], though no practical algorithm seems to be known;
    skewness has also been shown to be xed-parameter tractable on arbitrary xed
    surfaces [245]. Convex skewness is xed-parameter tractable [373, Section 5.1].
Relationships: sk(G) ⩽ cr(G), and for every k there are 1-skew graphs with crossing
    number k [237]; there is a bound cr(G) ⩽ (3 sk(G)2 + (4n − 17) sk(G))/6 which is
    tight for innitely many 1-skew graphs [279, 281]. It is known that sk(G) = cr(G) if
    and only if lcr(D) ⩽ 1 for every crossing-minimal drawing D of G [279]. For surfaces
    we have skΣ (G) ⩽ crΣ (G). sk(G) ⩾ γ(G), where γ(G) is the orientable genus of
    G (which equals the bundled crossing number bc′ (G)), and the toroidal grid shows
    that there are toroidal graphs with arbitrary large skewness. sk(G) ⩽ ecr(G) (by
    denition), and a planar grid with an additional edge shows that there are 1-skew
    graphs with arbitrarily large ecr. sk(G) ⩾ m−g/(g−2) (n−2), where g is the girth of
    G, and, more generally, skΣ (G) ⩾ m − g/(g − 2) (n − 2 + 2γ(Σ)) [413, 507]. sk(G) =
    O((∆γ(G)n)1/2 ) [282, 284]. If G is 1-planar, then sk(G) ⩽ n − 2 [256] (also see
    Footnote 157). If G has a drawing with c crossings and skewness k , then cr2k (G) ⩽
    c [271]. Kainen [508] showed that χ(G) ⩾ r implies that sk(G) ⩾ sk(Kr ), an early
  160 In the entry on nodal crossing number, we suggest the name vertex-skewness for this notion. The

of generalized vertex-skewness [543].

Space crossing number
Definition: A spatial drawing of a graph G is a continuous embedding of G in R3 , it is
      rectilinear if edges are line segments. A spatial crossing is any (straight) line that
      crosses four 164 vertex-disjoint edges. The space crossing number of G, space-cr(G), is
      the smallest number of spatial crossings in any spatial drawing of G. The rectilinear
      space crossing number, space-cr(G), is the smallest number of spatial crossings in
    any rectilinear spatial drawing of G.
Reference: Bukh, Hubard [172].
Comments: For a notion of crossing number for geometric hypergraphs, see [60, 61].
Complexity: Open.
                                  cr(G)
                                          ; for every k there is a graph G with space-cr(G) =
                                        
Relationships: space-cr(G) ⩽        2
    0 and cr(G) ⩾ k [172]. There is a crossing lemma, space-cr(G) ⩾ m6 /(cn4 log2 n)
    for c = 4179 , and n = |V |, m = |E| as long as m ⩾ 441 n [172].
Open Questions: Bukh and Hubbard ask whether graphs with space-cr(G) = 0 are
    minor-closed and whether space-cr(G) = 0 is equivalent to space-cr(G) = 0. They
    conjecture negative answers in both cases.
Also see: Grid crossing number.


Spherical crossing number. See geodesic crossing number.
Spherical arc crossing number
Definition: In a spherical arc drawing of a graph G the vertices of G lie in general
    position on the unit sphere S 2 and edges are geodesics (which are unique since the
    vertices are in general position). The spherical arc crossing number of a graph G is
    the smallest number of crossings in a spherical arc drawing of G.
Reference: Based on Streltsova, Wagner [779, 780].
Comments: Streltsova and Wagner [779, 780] introduce the spherical arc crossing num-
    ber of the complete graph to prove Hill's conjecture for sperical arc drawings.
Complexity: ∃R-complete (adapting [135]).
Relationships: The spherical arc crossing number of G lies between crS 2 (G), which
    does not require general position, and cr(G).
Values: The value of the spherical arc crossing number for Kn is Z(n) = 1/4 ⌊n/2⌋⌊(n −
    1)/2⌋⌊(n − 2)/2⌋⌊(n − 3)/2⌋.
Open Questions: Does the spherical arc crossing number equal the spherical crossing
    number?
Also see: Spherical crossing number (under geodesic crossing number), rectilinear cross-
    ing number.
  164 Bukh and Hubbard also, in passing, mention the possibility of counting lines that cross three edges.




the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                  95
Spine crossing number
Definition: The spine crossing number 165 of G in a book of k pages is the smallest num-
    ber of crossings between edges and the spine in a k -page topological book embedding
    of G. In a topological book embedding edges are allowed to cross the spine.
Reference: Based on Miyauchi [610].
Comments: Miyauchi gives an upper bound on the number of spine crossings for Kn in
    a 3-page book (also see discussion in the entry on book crossing number).
Complexity: Open.
Relationships: Any graph G = (V, E) has a k + 1-page topological book embedding
    in which each edge crosses the spine at most ⌈logk |V |⌉ times, so the spine crossing
    number of a graph G = (V, E) in a (k + 1)-page book is at most |E|⌈logk |V |⌉ [310,
    609], and this bound is tight [311]. If G is planar, then the spine crossing number
    in a 2-page book is at most |E|, by a result of Kaufmann and Wiese [530]. 166
Values: Graphs with spine crossing number zero in 1-page book are exactly the sub-
    graphs of outerplanar graphs, and in 2-page books, the subgraphs of planar Hamil-
    tonian graphs.
Also see: Book crossing number


Stable crossing number
Definition: The stable crossing number of G with parameter k is crΣ (G) where Σ =
    Sγ(G)−k and γ(G) is the (orientable) genus of G.
Reference: Kainen [511].
Comments: Kainen's motivation in introducing the stable crossing number seems to
    have been to investigate innite families of graphs in surfaces in which they are
    nearly embeddable and show that this can lead to small constant (stable) crossing
    numbers [511, Abstract].
Complexity: NP-complete even for k = 1, since determining the planar crossing num-
    ber of a toroidal graph is NP-complete, e.g. by the result of Cabello, Mohar [181].
Values: crΣ (Kn ) = 6k (resp. crΣ (Kn ) = 6k − 3) for Σ = Sγ(Kn )−k for 1 ⩽ k ⩽ ⌈n/12⌉
    if n ≡ 0 mod 12 (resp. n ≡ 6 mod 12) [786]. 4k ⩽ crΣ (Qn ) ⩽ 8k for Σ = Sγ(Qn )−k
    and 0 ⩽ k ⩽ γ(Qn ) [511]. crΣ (Qn □ K4,4 ) = 4k , where 0 ⩽ k ⩽ 2n , Σ =
    Sγ(Qn □K4,4 )−k [517]; for a generalization of this result, see [685, Theorem 9].
Open Questions: Kainen [511] conjectured crΣ (Qn ) = 8k for Σ = Sγ(Qn )−k .

ings over the spine. It has also been studied as a minimization problem for upward planar drawings [598].
  166 Kaufmann and Wiese show that every planar graph has a 2-page drawing in which every edge crosses

String crossing number
Definition: The string crossing number of G, str-cr(G), is the smallest number of cross-
    ings in any string drawing of G minus |E(G)|. A string drawing of G is a set of
    curves (cv )v∈V (G) so that cu and cv cross for every edge uv ∈ E(G). 167
Reference: Bokal, Czabarka, Székely, Vr´o [151].
Comments: Bokal, Czabarka, Székely, Vr´o [151] also suggest the independent string
    crossing number (they call it the faithful crossing number) and the pair string cross-
    ing number; string graphs correspond to graphs of pair string crossing number 0.
    Richter, Thomassen [705] study a similar notion for closed curves in their proof that
    cr(C5 □ C5 ) = 15. One could also imagine a local string crossing number, which
    counts crossings along the strings. For (strict) string representations, this has been
    studied [225, 561].
Complexity: Open.
Relationships: str-crΣ (G) ⩽ 4 mcrΣ (G) [151].


Surface crossing number. See crossing number and stable crossing number.
t-circle crossing number
Definition: A t-circle drawing of a graph G is a drawing in which the vertices of G lie on
     t disjoint circles which are empty; that is, the face bounded by each circle contains
     no part of G or any other circle. The t-circle crossing number, crt◦ (G), of a graph
     G is the smallest number of crossings in a t-circle drawing of G. For a t-partite
     graph G with a xed partition, a t-partite circle drawing is a t-circle drawing of G
     in which the vertices of each part of G lie on the same, distinct circle. The t-partite
     circle crossing number, cr t (G), is the smallest number of crossings in a t-partite
     circle drawing of G (for a given partition). The 2-partite circle crossing number is
     also known as the bipartite cylindrical crossing number.
Reference: Duque, González-Aguilar, Hernández-Vélez, Leaños, Medina [295]. The bi-
     partite cylindrical crossing number was introduced by Ábrego, Fernández-Merchant,
     and Sparks [14], there written as cr⊚ ; the tripartite circle crossing number was in-
     troduced in [185].
Comments: The 1-circle crossing number is the same as the 2-page crossing number.
     2-partite circle drawings are more commonly known as bipartite cylindrical draw-
     ings [706], and this is where this family of crossing numbers originated. From there
     the cylindrical crossing number, and then the t-circle crossing number developed.
     The notation cr t for the t-partite circle crossing number was introduced in [185].
     If we x the cyclic order of the vertices on the circles, we obtain the map cross-
     ing number. A practical crossing minimization studied in [783] can be viewed as a
     variant of the 2-circle crossing number problem with constraints on lengths of edges.
  167 Crossings between c
                            u and cv are allowed even if there is no edge uv ; so a string drawing is not a
string representation in the strict sense in which a string graph is the intersection graph of a set of curves
in the plane.

      3 [295].    Testing cr t (G) ⩽ k is NP-complete for every xed t ⩾ 2. 169

t-polygonal crossing number
Definition: The t-polygonal crossing number of G, crt (G), is the smallest number of
     crossings in a straight-line drawing of G in which every edge is allowed to consist of
     up to t line segments.
Reference: Bienstock [135].
Comments: Introduced by Bienstock [135] to bridge the gap between cr and cr. In
     the area of graph drawing, t-polygonal drawings would also be called (t − 1)-bend
     drawings (each edge having at most t − 1 bends).
Complexity: ∃R-complete [135] for t = 1, see [729] for ∃R. Open for t > 1.
Relationships: cr1 (G) = cr(G) (by denition), cr2 (G) ⩽ 2 cr(G)2 [136]. Let t(k) be
     the smallest t so that crt (G) = cr(G) for all G with cr(G) ⩽ k . Then t(k) =
     Θ(k 1/2 ) [135].
Also see: Rectilinear crossing number.


Tile crossing number
Definition: A tile T       is a graph G = (V, E) together with two disjoint sequences L =
    {u1 , . . . , uk } and R = {v1 , . . . , vk } of vertices in V . A tile drawing of T is a drawing
    of T in the unit square with all vertices of L on the left boundary of the square in
    order, that is, ui above ui+1 , and all vertices of R on the right boundary with vi
    above vi+1 . The tile crossing number of T is the smallest number of crossings in a
    tile drawing of T . T 2 is the tile obtained from T by placing two copies of T next to
    each other and identifying vi of the left copy with ui of the right copy, for 1 ⩽ i ⩽ k .
    This denes tiles T n for arbitrary integer powers n. The average crossing number of
    T is the limit of the tile crossing number of T n divided by n as n goes to innity. 170
Reference: Pinontoan, Richter [689].

  168 The reduction is from bkcr (G) = 0, which is not known to be hard for t = 3. The problem remains

  170 This is not to be confused with the average crossing number in knot theory, which is dened for an
embedded knot; there is a graph-theoretic version of that crossing number, but it is for xed straight-line

embeddings of graphs in R      [390].




the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                   98
Comments: Pinontoan and Richter [689] do not require that |L| = |R|, but they mostly
    study tiles they call self-compatible for which this is the case, since for those tiles the
    average crossing number is dened. They can show that the average crossing number
    of a tile always exists. The tile crossing number is rather specic to constructions of
    crossing-critical graphs. It bears similarity to bipartite and convex crossing number,
    but diers from them by allowing additional vertices within the square. In that
    respect, it resembles the anchored crossing number most closely. For an extension
    of the tile crossing number, the panel crossing number, see [518].
Complexity: The tile crossing number is NP-complete, 171 and remains NP-complete
    for twisted planar tiles (tiles which become planar after twisting one of the bound-
    aries) [459]. If L ∪ R = V , then the problem is in polynomial time. The complexity
    of the average crossing number is open, but Dvo°ák and Mohar [297] show that it
    can be approximated in exponential time in the absolute error.
Relationships: tile-cr(T n ) ⩽ n tile-cr(T ) [689]. Let o(T n ) be the graph constructed
    from T n by identifying L and R of the tile T n (in order). Then the average crossing
    number of T equals limn→∞ cr(o(T n ))/n [689].
Open Questions: Pinotoan and Richter [689] conjecture that if the average crossing
    number of T equals tile-cr(T ), then there is an N so that cr(o(T n ))/n = tile-cr(T )
    for all n ⩾ N . ▼ Dvo°ák and Mohar [297] conjecture that the average crossing
    number of a tile is always a rational number.
Also see: Anchored crossing number (under xed linear crossing number), bipartite

Triple crossing number
Definition: The triple crossing number of G, triple-cr(G), is the smallest number of
    triple crossings (a point in which three edges cross) in a drawing in which there
    are only triple crossings. We assume that there are no self-crossings, no crossings
    between adjacent edges, and that independent edges cross at most once and do not
    touch. The triple crossing number may be innite.
Reference: Tanaka, Teragaito [797].
Comments: As the denition shows, Tanaka, Teragaito [797] introduce a very restrictive
    version of a triple crossing number (which more accurately could be called the simple
    triple crossing number). In this version, triple-cr(K5 ) = ∞, since crossings have
    to occur between independent edges (forcing at least 6 endpoints in a non-planar
    graph). However, it it easy to give a drawing of K5 with two triple crossings if
    crossings between adjacent edges are allowed. Another condition that could be
    relaxed is that independent edges cross at most once. Tanaka and Teragaito in
    passing also introduce the n-fold crossing number. Harborth [431, 435] studied
    multiple crossings (see Footnote 92).
  171 The regular crossing number is a special case for k = 0.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                       99
Complexity: Open.
Relationships: cr(G) ⩽ 3 triple-cr(G) (perturb triple crossings).          The triple crossing
    number is not monotone (for example, triple-cr(K4,4 ) = ∞, while triple-cr(K6,4 ) =
    4 [797].
Values: Tanaka and Teragaito [797] determine the triple crossing number for all com-
    plete k -partite graphs; in particular, they show that triple-cr(Kn ) = ∞ for n ⩾
    5, and triple-cr(Km,n ) = ∞ for non-planar Km,n with the following exceptions:
    triple-cr(K3,3 ) = triple-cr(K3,4 ) = 1, triple-cr(K3,6 ) = 2, and triple-cr(K4,6 ) = 4.
Also see: Degenerate crossing number.

Tutte crossing number. See algebraic crossing number.
Uncrossed crossing number
Definition: A collection of drawings of G is uncrossed if every edge of G is crossing-free
    in at least one
                  P of the drawings. The uncrossed crossing number, ucr(G), of G is
    the smallest D∈D cr(D) over all uncrossed collections D of drawings of G. If D is
    restricted to consist of at most k drawings, one gets the uncrossed crossing number
    in at most k drawings, denoted ucrk (G).
Reference: Hlin¥ny and Masa°ík [460].
Comments: Hlin¥ny and Masa°ík [460] also introduce two new parameters: unc(G), the
    uncrossed number, the smallest k for which there is an uncrossed collection of k
    drawings for G, and ounc(G), the crossing-optimal uncrossed number, the smallest
    k for which ucr(G) = ucrk (G).
Complexity: Testing whether ucr(G) ⩽ m and ucr2 (G) ⩽ m are both NP-complete
    and testing ucrk (G) ⩽ m is xed-parameter tractable in parameter m [460].
Relationships: For every m there is a graph G with cr(G) = 1 and ucr(G) ⩾ m [460].
    There is a crossing lemma: ucr(G) ⩾ m4 /(87n3 ) [460]. unc(G) ⩾ ⌈m/(m −
    ecr(G))⌉ [199].
Values: ucr(Kn ) ∈ Θ(n5 ) [460].
Open Questions: The uncrossed crossing number of a graph lies between its thickness
    and its outerthickness. Can it dier arbitrarily from its outerthickness [100]?
Also see: Non-crossing edge number (under edge crossing number).

Upward crossing number
Definition: A drawing is monotone if every vertical line in the plane intersects each
      edge at most once. The upward crossing number of a directed acyclic graph G is the
      smallest number of crossings in a monotone drawing of G in which all edges point in
      the same direction. We write mon-cr⪯ (G), where ⪯ is the partial ordering induced
      by the orientation of G. For mixed graphs, containing both directed and undirected
      edges, the mixed upward crossing number is the smallest number of crossings in a
      monotone drawing of G in which all directed edges point in the same direction. The
      upward local crossing number, lcr↑ (G), of a directed acyclic graph G is the smallest
      k such that G has a monotone drawing with at most k crossings along each edge.

the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                     100
Reference: The upward crossing number is based on Eiglsperger, Kaufmann [306], also
    Chimani, Zeranski [223]. The upward local crossing number was introduced by
    Angelini et al. [55].
Comments: The upward crossing number is one of the monotone crossing numbers.
    It corresponds to the layer-free upward crossing minimization problem [216]. Ei-
    glsperger and Kaufmann dene the notion of a crossing number for a (mixed) up-
    ward planarization, calling it the (mixed) upward crossing minimal problem. Chi-
    mani and Zeranski [223] then use the term upward crossing number. The upward
    crossing number could also be called the directed crossing number or the hierarchical
    crossing number; the latter term has been used in the context of leveled graphs [629].
    Generalizing to recurrent hierarchies, One could dene a clockwise crossing number
    (see cyclic level crossing number). The upward local crossing number extends the
    local crossing number to directed graphs; the paper [55] also looks at the upward lo-
    cal crossing number in convex drawings, but does not introduce a separate crossing
    number notion for this.
Complexity: Even testing whether a graph is upward planar, that is, has upward cross-
    ing number 0, is NP-complete [379]. See [224] for a survey on upward planarity
    testing, and [223] for a survey on exact upward crossing minimization. Testing
    lcr↑ (G) ⩽ 1 is NP-complete even for graph classes for which upward planarity
    testing can be performed in polynomial time [55].
Relationships: mon-cr(G) ⩽ mon-cr⪯ (G), where ⪯ is the partial ordering induced by
    the orientation of G. The bimodal crossing number is a lower bound on mon-cr⪯ (G).
    lcr↑ (G) ⩽ φ(G)2 [55], and lcr↑ (G □ H) ∈ O(max(φ(G), φ(H)) [356], where φ is
    the bandwidth. Angelini et al. [55] note that a lcr↑ -minimal drawing may require
    crossings between incident edges (similar to the local crossing number); this is not
    the case for lcr↑ (G) ⩽ 3, the proof that lcr(G) = lcr∗ (G) for lcr(G) ⩽ 3 works for
    monotone drawings.
Open Questions: Computing the upward crossing number remains NP-complete even
    if we restrict the number of levels at which vertices can be placed: for two levels,
    the NP-complete bipartite crossing number is a special case. Is upward planarity
    xed-parameter tractable if the parameter is the number of levels? ▼ Is there a
    function f and a constant ε > 0 such that every n-vertex planar graph G with
    maximum degree ∆ satises lcr↑ (G) = O(f (∆)n1−ε ) [55].
Also see: Monotone crossing numbers, bimodal crossing number, bipartite crossing
    number, clockwise crossing number (under cyclic level crossing number).

Upward local crossing number. See upward crossing number.
Weighted crossing number
Definition: The weighted crossing number, cr(D, w) of a drawing D of a graph G =
      (V, E) with weights w : E 2 → R⩾0 , is dened as e,f ∈E w(e, f ) · iD (e, f ), where
                                                         P
      iD (e, f ) is the number of crossings between e and f in D. The weighted crossing
      number, cr(G, w) is the minimum of cr(D, w) over all drawings of G. The weighted

the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                  101
      rectilinear crossing number, cr(G, w) is the minimum of cr(D, w) over all straight-
    line drawings D of G.
Reference: Jackson, Ringel [501], Mohar [612], Schaefer, Sedgwick, tefankovi£ [734].
    Biedl, Chimani, Derka, Mutzel [134] for the rectilinear variant.
Comments: Assigning weights to edges (as opposed to edge pairs) is an old idea. Integer
    weights are typically interpreted as parallel copies of simple edges; for many crossing
    number variants, it is easy to show that k parallel edges correspond to a single edge
    of weight k . This argument may have rst occurred in a paper by Kainen [507] in
    which he shows that crΣ (G) ⩽ k 2 crΣ (G′ ) where G is a graph with at most k parallel
    edges between every pair of vertices, and G′ is the underlying simple graph of G. If
    G has exactly k parallel edges between every pair of vertices, then equality holds.
    This shows, as Scheinerman and Ullman [740, Theorem 7.1.4] observed, that the
    fractional crossing number equals the crossing number and thus is of no independent
    interest. Jackson and Ringel explicitly introduce the problem for determining the
    weighted crossing number of complete bipartite graphs. Asiri et al. [77] consider a
    binary-weighted variant, and push the idea further by allowing constraints on the
    number of crossings in sets of pairs of edges. 172 A similar idea, in which edges
    are colored and crossings between pairs of edges having the same pair of colors, is
    studied in [245]. Some crossing number variants, like independent crossing number
    and the crossing number of abstract topological graphs, can be considered special
    cases of the weighted crossing number. Mohar and Stephen [617] study the expected
    value of randomly weighted graphs and derive a crossing lemma for this case. A
    special case based on partitioning edges into three (but it could be more) classes is
    introduced in [51] as hierarchical partial planarity.
Complexity: NP-complete [734]. 173 The problem remains NP-complete even if the
    underlying graph is a K3,n [134]. The weighted rectilinear crossing number problem
    is ∃R-complete (since cr is ∃R-complete).
Also see: Crossing number of abstract topological graph.


Wire crossing number
Definition: A layout is a partition of a rectangle (the chip area) into two types of smaller
    rectangles: modules, where wires end, and regions, through which wires are routed.
    Vertices are located on the boundary of modules. An edge between two vertices
    has associated with it the netlist, the list of regions it passes through (in the given
    order) to connect its endpoints. The wire crossing number is the smallest number
    of crossings with which all the netlists can be realized.
Reference: Based on Groenveld [401]. Also, Chen and Lee [201].

  172 This approach allows them to determine the crossing number of many Cartesian products of paths
with small graphs in a very uniform fashion.
  173 This assumes w is considered part of the input (so weights can be large). NP-hardness follows from
Garey, Johnson [378] since the regular crossing number is a special case. NP-membership is harder.




the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                102
Comments: The study of crossings numbers for VLSI layouts traces back to the work
    of Leighton [576],174 but after a while more specialized models developed. 175 The
    one described above is closest in spirit to Groenveld's description [401] and Chen
    and Lee's later version [201]. The name wire crossing number was not used in those
    papers, but rst appears, as far as we know, in [521], a paper that describes a slightly
    dierent model, and introduces the notion of hypercrossings, crossings of hyperedges
    (Groenveld [401] also considers hyperedges, multi-terminal nets in his terminology,
    but deals with them dierently). The wire crossing number as dened above is
    not particularly interesting as a graph crossing number, because the topology of
    the edges does not change (with respect to the modules). Any two edges cross at
    most once, and their isotopy class determines whether they have to cross or not.
    We decided to include the wire crossing number, since it contains aspects of several
    other crossing numbers: it is really a special case of the map crossing number or
    the constrained crossing number in which the isotopy type of each edge is xed.
    The idea of routing along given tracks (the netlists) is also similar to the Metro-
    line crossing number. Marek-Sadowska and Sarrafzadeh [588] also consider what
    Chen and Lee [201] call the unconstrained crossing minimization problem in which
    the isotopy type of the edges is not xed. Both papers claim a polynomial time
    algorithm for the problem in this case, which is unlikely, since the unconstrained
    version of the problem is equivalent to computing a map crossing number, which is
    NP-complete [676]. 176
Complexity: Polynomial time [401].
Relationships: Map crossing number, constrained crossing number, Metro-line crossing
    number.

x-monotone crossing number. See monotone crossing numbers.


4      Some New Questions on Crossing Numbers

Several open questions have already been embedded in the text above, we don't want to
repeat these here. The following questions, as far as we know, are new. For questions
from previous versions of the survey that have been answered, see the end of this section.
   It's common knowledge that adjacent crossings don't matter, so the following should
be easy:

Question 2. Is cr(Kn ) = cr− (Kn )?
    174 See Remark 5 on an early forerunner.
    175 We should mention that Hotz [485, Section 3.6] develops a notion of (hyperedge) crossing number
for circuit layout and poses at least one interesting special problem for the bipartite crossing number.
Unfortunately, he works over an abstract notion of circuits introduced using category theory, which makes
his text unnecessarily hard to read. His notation for the crossing number of a circuit computing a Boolean
function f is LV (f ).
    176 The two papers really show that one can eciently nd a drawing in which every pair of edges
crosses at most once. Such a drawing need not be crossing-minimal, of course.



the electronic journal of combinatorics (2026), #DS21                                                 103
   In reality, we do not even know whether there is a good bound on the total number of
crossings in a cr− -minimal drawing of Kn . There are many similar open questions for other
crossing numbers, for example, pcr(Kn ) = cr(Kn ) and ocr+ (Kn ) = ocr(Kn ) = iocr(Kn ).
For monotone crossing numbers some progress has been made [99].
   We know that the cr problem is ∃R-complete so, as Bienstock realized, optimal draw-
ings can require exponential precision in the coordinates. What happens if we only have
polynomial precision available?

Question 3. Is there a function f so that G has a straight-line grid drawing on a O(n) ×
O(n) grid (that is, vertices are grid points) with at most f (cr(G)) crossings?

    We can broaden the question by using the grid crossing number: is there a function f
so that cr# (G, nk , 2) ⩽ f (cr(G)) for some k ?
    One can also consider games as the source of crossing number denitions; here is a
pen and paper crossing game based on an idea from [625]:

Question 4. Suppose we arrange 2n points on the boundary of a disk; players alter-
nate connecting pairs of points; crossing your own edge costs two points, crossing your
opponent's edge costs one point. Who wins?

    A recent computer game [112] suggests a concrete notion of a game crossing number:

Question 5. Two players alternate placing vertices of a graph (a Cn in the original
game) for a straight-line drawing of the graph in the plane. A vertex once placed cannot
be moved. The rst player attempts to minimize the number of crossings, the second
player tries to maximize them. What is the largest number of crossings the second player
can force in the nal drawing?

    By Fary's theorem, cr(G) = 0 implies that cr(G) = 0. Does Fary's theorem generalize
to other crossing numbers? For most, it is either an immediate consequence (pair crossing
number, local crossing number) or irrelevant (bipartite and book crossing number, for
example). The answer is no for the simultaneous crossing number, since scr(T, P ) = 0
for any tree T and path P , and there are trees and paths for which scr(T, P ) > 0 [59].
What about metric surfaces other than the plane? To take the easiest open example:

Question 6. If a graph can be embedded in a torus, does it always have a geodesic
embedding in the torus?

    We assume the torus is a standard geometric torus with the natural distance met-
ric inherited from 3-dimensional space. There are related results by de Verdière [247],
Mohar [613] and Hubard, Kaluºa, de Mesmay and Tancer [491] for other metrics.

Remark 10 (More Open Questions). Lists of open questions can also be found in the book
by Brass, Moser, and Pach [163, Chapter 9] and articles by Pach and Tóth [667], Branden-

Some of these questions are no longer open.                                           ♦

4.1 Answers to Previous Open Questions
We previously asked how hard it is to determine cr(G) mod 2. The parity of the crossing
number of the complete graph was studied in papers by Guy [410], Kleitman [536, 537],177
Archdeacon, Richter, and others.
   Hlin¥ny and Thomassen [466] settled the question by showing that determining the
parity of cr(G) is NP-complete. They point out that their proof generalizes to cr and
any xed integer modulus.
